ON THE PARIS, LYONS AND MEDITERRANEAN RAILWAY TO THE SUNNY SOUTH OF FRANCE. The wretched weather that has prevailed throughout England for the past five months, has brought us an extraordinary number of enquiries through the various tourist agencies, for bookings to the South of France and Italy, and the uniform beautiful weather they have been enjoying in those countries would point to an unusually as well as a record season for the South. With their customary enterprise, the P.L.M. Railway Company have already issued booklets setting forth the coming winter's arrangements, and these can be procured gratis and post free by sending post card or letter to KNOTT, KITCHENER & Co. LIMITED (Tourist Department), 1, SOUTHAMPTON ROW, LONDON, W.C. With the extremely low rates now, fixed by the above company, few people will have any excuse to remain in England during the wintry fogs, sleet and rain.

COUNTY PALATINE OF CHESTER. NOTICE IS HEREBY GIVEN that the following Bye-laws made by the Cheshire County Council, at its meeting on the Ist day of August, 1912, in pursuance of the powers conferred upon them by the ADVERTISEMENTS REGULATION ACT. 1907 (7 Edwd. VII., ch. 27), having been approved by the Secretary of State, are now operative in the following Drban and Rural Districts in the Administrative County of Chester, viz.: URBAN DISTRICTS. Alderley Edge. Alsager. Ashton-upon-Mersey. Bebington Higher. Bollington. ' Bowdon. Bredbury and Romiley. Bromborough. Buglawton. Cheadle and Galley. Compstall. Hale. Handforth. Hazel Grove and Bramhall. Hollingworth. Hoole. Knutsf ord. Lymm. Marple. Middlewich. Mottram-in-Longdendale. Nantwich. Neston and Parkgate. Sandbach. Tarporley. Wilmslow. Yeardsley-cum-Whaley. RURAL DISTRICTS

Bucklow. - ----- Chester. Congleton. Disiey. Macclesfield. Malnas. Nautwich. Northwich. RUDCOM Tarvin. Tintwistle. Wirral. Nothing in Part I. of these Bye-laws shall apply to any hoarding or similar structure not exceeding 12 feet in height.

PART I. I.—Every person who shall intend to erect a hoard- ing or similar structure for advertising purposes or to use for advertising purposes any hoarding or similar structure not then used for such purpose., shall four- teen days at least before commencing such erection or use give notice in writing of his intention to the Council together with a plan sufficient to show the position of the hoarding or structure with regard to

===== 0002601_19121005_art0004.txt | included=true | included =====
the street and adjacent houses, if any, and a statement of the height of the boarding or structure and of the materials of which it is to be constructed. 2.—Every hoarding or similar structure used for advertising purposes and placed so near to any street or public place that it might if not supported fall thereon, shall be securely erected and maintained by the person erecting or having the use of the hoarding or structure as the case may be. 3.—Every person having the use of a hoarding or similar structure for advertising purposes shall exhibit and keep exhibited his name and address in legible charazters in a conspicuous position on the front of the hoarding or structure.

4.—The person having the use of a hoarding or similar structure for advertising purposes or the occupier of the land on which the hoarding or structure is erected (if the person having the use thereof cannot after diligent inquiry be found by the Council and the Council have served a notice on such occupier requiring him to comply with the provisions of this Bye-law) shall maintain the hoarding or structure in a neat and tidy condition, and if any paper or other material affixed thereto for advertising purposes becomes detached shall forthwith remove and clear away such paper or other material.

s.—No person shall on any ground adjoining a street erect any hoarding or similar structure for advertising purposes in front of the line of the front main wall of the houses, if any, within 30 feet on either side of such hoarding or structure, or use any such hoarding or structure so erected. 6.—No person shall erect any hoarding or similar structure for advertising purposes to a greater height than the height of the front main wall of the houses, if any, within 30 feet on either aide of such hoarding or structure, or use any such hoarding or structure so erected.

7.—No person shall erect any boarding or similar structure for advertising purposes in such a position as to reduce the open space in front of or in the rear of any inhabited building to less than the apace required by the Bye-laws relating to new streets and buildings for the time being in force, or to less than a clear distance of 30 feet, whichever may be the greater. PART 11. B.—Nothing in these Bye-laws shall apply to any hoarding or similar structure in use for advertising purposes at the date of the confirmation of these Bye- laws, or to any advertisment exhibited at such date, for a period of five years from that date, but at the expiration Of such period the Bye-laws shall apply to any such hoarding or structure or advertisement as if the same were to be first erected used or exhibited subsequently to such period and as if the person using the hoarding or structure were the person 'erecting or intending to erect or use the hoarding or structure. By Order. REGINALD POTTS, County Offices, Clerk to the County Council. 27th September, 1912. Northgateltreet, Chester.

INTOTICE.—WAR DEPARTMENT CONTRACT FOR BUILDING WORK AND REPAIRS in the Liverpool District of the Western Command. TENDERS are about to be asked for the "COMPLETION " of the TRIENNIAL CONTRACT for BUILDING WORKS and REPAIRS at ORFORD BARRACKS, WARRINGTON. PERIOD.—From NOVEMBER 6th 1912 to MARCH 31st. 1914. Contractors desirous of tendering, and not on the War Department Lists should apply by letter to the COMMANDING ROYAL ENGINEER, R.E. OFFICE, 14, ELLIOT STREET, LIVERPOOL, stating at the same time the name and address of Bankers, and of two Architects (or Engineers) under whom they have recently carried out work, as bona Jules of their business standing. A Copy of the Current Schedule of Prices may be seen on application, or purchased for the sum of 15s. W. P. BRETT, Colonel. C.R.E. Liverpool District.

THREE LUCKY ENGLISHMEN.

rilliE Statement that British Subjects never win any- thing with. Foreign Bonds has received another startling contradiction, for at the drawing of Hungarian Foncier 1905 Bonds held at Bnda Pest on August 26th,the FIRST PRIZE OF £18,500

, - was won by a gentleman residing at Taunton; the Second Prize was secured by a Retired Spa Captain in Cornßall ; and the third and fourth prizes both went to a gentleman residing at Bushy Heath. All these Bonds were sold by

CUNLIFFE, RUSSELL Sz CO., of 10 & 12, PLACE DE LA BOURSE, PARIS. The first-mentioned Bond had only been fully paid for in May last, and on the second Bond several instal- ments still remained to be paid, whereby the assertion that their Customers frequently win Prizes during the time they are making their remittances receives new confirmation. Hungarian Fonder 1906 Bonds take part in six drawings each year at which drawings enormous sum of money are distributed—indeed in December 1910 another Client of Messrs. CUNLIFFE, RUSSELL SI-. CO. (also a British Subject) drew Forty-one thousand Two Hundred and Fifty Pounds sterling, which amount will again be drawn for next December.

JOHN WALKER & CO., LTD. PRINTERS, BOOKBINDERS, &C. EXAMINER OFFICE. TEL. 3551.

HOCKEY.

LATCHFORD. The first team bad Winnington Park AS visitors and the latter won a close game by 2-1. Winnington got the first goal following a penalty corner and then the home forwards had hard luck when two shots struck the posts. The visitors kept the lead until well on in the second half. Pearson equalised with a character- istic effort and followed it up with another great shot that was kept, out of the net by Mottershead with the utmost difficulty, and hereabouts Latchford pressed very hard. Winningtora's second goal should not ;aye been allowed because one of the borne defenders in clearing had his stick over-checked (a palpable foul). Meanwhile the ball was picked up by one of the attackers and put into the net; the incident escaped the notice of the referee. Fletcher played a fine game for Latchford at right back and there was really not much wrong with the defence, bnt the forwards were disappointing.altbough allowance must be made for the soundness of the Winnington backs. The second team retired beaten by 5-1 at Winnington. Minards scored for Latchford with a capital shot but Winnington led 2-1 at the interval and, taking every advantage of the chances that came their way, put on another three goals in the second half The score hardly ere presents the balance of play in favour of Winnington and the visitors were handicapped by an injury to one of their halves. Bradshaw worked very hard at centre-half. The forwards lacked under- standing with one another but the team generally is not having the best of luck. There will no doubt be an improvement when the new players have settled down. The First Team v Bootle at Ilcotle this afternoon is: If Wright, goal ; W Fletcher, J B Wilkinson, backs; W Isherwood, A Knight, H Lees, halves; R A Dale, J E Pearson, E Banks, C Hackney, A Starkey, forwards. Train from Central Station 1.50 p.m. The Second Eleven v Bowdon "B "at home is: Bruit], H T Gibbs, T Dixon, backs; W Eccles, A E Bradshaw, T Banks, halves ; H R Colton, C Billington, R L Jones, W H Minards, H Eletchar, forwards. Bully-off 2.30 p.m. Umpire, Mr 10 Grounds.

LARGESSE AT MUSIC HALLS.

When a young clerk. named John Bampson, was tined at Old-street Police Court for disorderly conduct at the Shoreditch Empire. the manager said that the practice of throwing copper coins at the artists was becoming an abominable nuisance. Prisoner, who denied throwing more than one coin, said he intended it as a compli- ment, but the manager remarked that it was not taken as such.

NOTES BY " MUST."

Warrington have won, and not only won but indulged in quite a glut of scoring at Irwell-lane. Prior to meeting Runcorn they had scored one solitary try in four matches, and earned one solitary point out of a possible eight. On Saturday they lasted the joys of victory and scored thirteen points all in one after- noon. It was quite a noteworthy performance under the circumstances.

Right from its selection I had more hope of the re-arranged team than any side which has represented Warrington this season. There were fewer weak spots in the back division, and the experiment of playing Tranter and Brooks in the three-quarter line had possibilities. I may say at once that the experiments were an unqualified success. 'Tranter surprised everybody— except your humble servant--with his display at centre tbreequarter. I recommended his having a trial here last season, and it is admitted now that the advice was good. Tranter has all the makings of a fine centre. Be is strong and speedy, and be uses his bead. The way he gave the dummy and cut in for Warrington's first try would have done credit to such an old hand as George Dickenson himself. In fact, there were a few stalwarts at Runcorn who thought young 'rranter's style and methods were strongly reminiscent of Dickenson in his early days. I have great hopes of this young player. \Vhat be requires now is special training at a quick start and the sprint. If he can pick up another five yards in the hundred he will trouble the best men he metts.

Brooks also played with much of his old cleverness and dash. Be was far more of a success on the wing than at half, and made one iun on 6aturday which was brilliant. Unfortunately for him most of the play went to the other wing, where Mclntyre was not quite so reliable or speedy. True, he gut a capital try, but most of the work had been done by Tranter. A word shoulo be said for Dickenson, who was safety itself and took more than his share of the knocks. He is a bit slow on the attack, but few men can play for keeps better.

Now, as to half back. Well, the display of Rushton and Renwick um mixed. Neither was a success in the first half, but afterwards they combined to some purpose. Rushton was very nippy in-getting away with the ball in the second half and made two beautiful openings, while Renwick showed more dash than heretofore. Both were a bit weak in tackling at times, and it was lucky than the centres immediately behind were men with a etrong grip. On the attack Rushton gave his smartest di,play, but he did some wild things with the ball on hi a own goal-line. He must learn to stick tight in his own quarter, or at any rate not pass till he is sure the ball is going to a Warrington man. Three times on Saturday he threw the ball away within five yards of the line, and only some rematkable fumbling by the Runcorn backs saved a score.

The game itself was like all games between such keen local rivals—full of desperate rushing and deadly tackling. Runcorn ale never easy victims, and points are never cheap at Irwell-lane. They played a plucky game in the first half against a strong breeze. War- rington had to defend almost continuously, and a fine defence they put up, but it was the poor combination of the home backs which caused chances to go a-begging. I have seldom seen more passes drooped near the line. Consequently, Warrington turned round with the modest lead of a goal— kicked by Jolley from over half way—to nil.

At half-time the odds were at least 6 to 4 on Runcorn, and when Richardson equalised six minutes from the restart Warrington's chances seemed remote. It was from this point that a change came over the game. The Warrington forwards rose to the occasion in wonderful style, and quite outclassed the home six. Then the backs began to throw the ball about with some freedom, and after a desim'ate effort by Brooks, Tranter got in on the other side, Renwick adding a goal. Runcorn were well beaten from now to the close, and Warrington showed the best combination of the ieiaon. Obviously the passing game paid—as it always does if the men possess judgment and speed. Probably the fact that punting against the wind was hopelets had a lot to do with Warrington's changed tactics, but they were only playing the game they ought always to play. It cannot be too often emphasised that punting is merely a means of. giving the other side possession of the ball. Tode the Australasians !

The full back honours at any rate went to Runcorn, for whom Shaw played a very iesourceful game. He was better than Jolley on the day's play, though the young Wirepuller did some fine work on the defence.

What a difference that one win has made. Why, Warrington are only two places below Oldham, and not at all in bad company. I bope they have turned the corner to some purpose. But we shall learn more about their strength and weakness this afternoon.

With Wigan as visitors to-day Warrington look like having their best match under ideal conditions— I am venturing to presume that the fine weather will continue. They should be assured of a " bumper " gate, and I estimate that last Saturday's win was worth at least 5.50 to the club. Interest has been revived, and possibilities of a triumph over the Wiganera are being freely discussed. It is rather a large order to expect Warrington to win on current form, but the team is improving, is well trained and keen. As I suggest, there are possibilities.

Almost a t the last minute Warrington have strengthened the team by the introduction of two new forwards from Ebbw Vale,who aro both thirteen stone men. When I say strengthened lam taking the men on their face value. At any rate they are deemed good enough to turn out against Wigan, which is a severe test. Their names are A. Cox and R. Thomas, the first-named being 24 years old and the second 26 years. lithe second Thomas is as good as the first cne Warrington g.)t from Wales, then he will be an acquisition.

No change has been made in the back division, and I do not know that one could be recommended. The home crowd will be interested in seeing Tranter, Brooks and Renwick in their new positions. The full team is :

Jolley, full back; Brooks, Dickenson, Tranter and Mclntyre, threequarters ; Rushton and Renwick, half backs : Thomas, Cheaters, Skelhorne, Bevan, Cox aud E Thomas, forwards,

For this match I should have preferred Fearniey to Bevan, though the young Wiganer gave a good display last week.

The Wigan Committee /have made several changes in their team since last week. Gleave, Ramsdale and Sharrock return to the side, but Winstanley is omitted frcm the forwards for Silcock, and Miller is deposed, the team being : Sharrock, full back ; Bradley, Jenkins,Price, Todd, threequarters ; Gleave, Thomas, half backs ; Seeling, Ramsdale, Francis, Coldrick, Williams, Silcock, forwares.

Warrington should hardly need instructing in the policy to be adopted against Wigan. But there are two things to be always borne in mind. One is to keep both eyes on Johnny Thomas, and both hands wherever possible. The other is to kick away from Sharrock. The Wigan full back is very heavy this Reason and if he has to run for the ball the fast Warrington forwards will have a big chance of making a capture. Jolley needs to keep the touch- line busy as he did against Broughton.

The less said about the "A " team after last Satur- day and the better. There were two many novices in the ride, and even Runcorn's juniors out-classed them. A much stronger team has been gA together for the contest at Wigan, and the selection is ae follows ;

Brown, full hack ; Twist, Hudson, Carter, and Waywell, threequarters ; Jordan and Daintith, half backs; Fearnley, Critchlev, Cooper, Naylor, Fletcher and Gibbs, forwards.

The last has been seen of Jack Moore, the ex-Batley centre, and I am informed that he will be placed on the transfer list. Woodburn is a very probable starter next week.

Warrington's Welcome Win.

Warrington recorded their first win of the season on Saturday, when they achieved a fine victory over Runcorn on the Chesnire Club's ground. Warrington's experimental side shaped well, but Newell and Sutton, the two new men in the Runcorn ranks were not success. Fine weather favoured the fixture, and Mere was a crowd uf fatly 3,000 spectators. Teams : Runcorn.—Shaw, back ; Hughes, Moran, Moores and Newell, three-quarter backs ; Steele and Sutton, half backs ; Inman, Richardson, Massey, Prince, Peacock, and Stevenson, forwards. Warrington.—Jolley, back ; Brookes, Dickenson, 'fruiter, and Mclntyre, three-quarter backs ; Renwick land Ruston, half backs ; Thomas, Cheaters, Skelhorne, Fearnley, Bevan, and Critchley, forwards. Referee, A Smith, Halifax ; touch judges, A Smith, Leigh, and W Wallwork, Swinton. Warrington won the toss and opened with the advan- tage of a strong wind behind them. The early stages of the game favoured Warrington, and Renwick broke away and threw to Tranter, who was grassed near the home line. Runcorn rallied, and by dashing play collared Jolley in the Warrington quarter, where Inman took a sbot at goal, but failed owing to the wind. Warrington were bard pressed, and Sutton gave Moores a beautiful pass a couple of yards from the line, but the chance was mulled badly, and Warrington cleared. Runcorn got offside at centre, and Jolley opened the score with a fine goal kicked from over half-way. Undaunted, the home forwards again rushed the ball to the Warrington quarter, and scrum after scrum was fought out near the line. The ball was passed out smartly to Newall, who failed to gather when challented by Brooks. Rushton kicked clear and the wind assisted to carry the ball nearly the length of the ground. Shaw shone with a dodgy run, and again sent play back to the Warrington and. Hughes sprinted away on the bottom wing and passed to Massey. The latter failed to reach the leather and Jolley gathered it from his toes very cleverly and returned up the field. Steele stole away from behind the pack adroitly, but Moran was palled up in time. Runcorn continued to monopolize the attack and Massey forced Mclntyre to find touch near his own line to save. From the succeed- ing scrimmages Moores and Stevenson both came within an ace of scoring. Rushton came to the rescue of his side again and a big kick was followed by the "downing ' of Shaw in the Runcorn half. A sharp sprint by Moran immediately removed the venue and another attack was speedily initiated. seen scrimmag- ing saw the ball carried over the Warrington line where Rushton took big risks in passing back, Dickinson being lucky to save under the posts. A bout of neat passing by Tranter and Mclntyre was broken up by Moran throwing the wing man out of play. Half- time : Warrington one goat (two points); Runcorn nil. Warrington reopened the game with much vigour, and Thomas made a dashing effort, which Shaw checkmated near the corner. Runcorn forced a way to the quarter flagoind Moran went, for the line in determined fashion, but was pulled down by Brookes and Tranter. War- rington were penalized for not playing the ball, and Richardson equalised with a penalty goal for Runcorn, amidst cheers. Warrington immediately took up the running, and Renwick, Dickenson and Mclntyre joined in a dangerous round of passing. It seemed that the wing man would go cver, but Shaw, who was playing a great game, stopped him near the corner flag. Hard scrimmaging followed in the vicinity of the Runcorn line, and Brookes dodged through the home backs cleverly. He got over, but was prevented from ground- ing the ball—a piece of hard luck for the visitors. Shaw got the ball away well, but the fact that War- rington were now getting possession from the pack counted for much. Relief was obtained when Jolley was downed by the Runcorn forwards well in his own half. From here Runcorn again travelled to the Warrington line and Newell went for the corner flag pluckily, Brookes pushing him out of play. The Warrington defence was sound, and the forwards dribbled to the centre, where Thomas picked up and threw to Rushton. The latter sent on to Tranter, who ran with great pace and romped over after giving Moores "the dummy." Jolley failed from a difficult position. Runcorn were ' the aggressive force immediately after this, and ' Renwick knocked on deliberately to spoil a passing movement of the home backs. He was promptly penalised and Richardson kicked a goal. With only one point separating the teams play was very keen. Inman dribbled right to the Warrington posts, where Jolley saved by throwing himself on the ball. A moment later Richardson was almost over. Runcorn seemed fated not to get through. After Inman had again shown good footwork Shaw tried a drop at goal, but the leather went wide. 'Renwick broke away from behind the pack , and after a strong run threw to Rushton, who scored under the bar. Renwick kicked a goal. Warrington followed up the success by attacking with great determination. Rushton initiated a clever movement which led Tranter I to" draw" the opposition and feed Mclntyre. The wing dashed through the borne defenders and registered a good try near the corner. Renwick was unable to improve. Runcorn were not entirely out of the picture, and Suttcn Steele and Moores handled well, the baler running right to the line, where Renwick pulled him down. The danger was temporarily removed. but Run- corn were soon back, and Sutton nipped in with a try just at the close. Richardson failed to land the goal. Result :—Warrington, two goals three tries (13 points); Runcorn, two goal one try (seven points). WIDNES LUCKY AT COVENTRY. Played at Coventry, in a high wind, before 1,500 spectators. Both teams gave a fine exposition, the boo e team never playing better. Their three-quarter line proved strong. Dakin and Davies, inside, playing brilliantly. The forwards, however, were not equal to the other side, and did poorly in the scrummages. Coventry found a strong force against them at times,

but their tackling was good. The home team led at half-time, and ten minutes before the close the score stood 11-7 in their favour. Then Higgins and Jones made 5 "points—as they had early on—and gave the visitors 1 point on the balance. Lally scored a goal for Widnes. On the other side, Johnston (2) and Deakin scored tries, and Carter a goal. Result :—Widnes 3 goals, 2 tries (12) points, Coventry 1 goal, 3 tries 11 points. ST. HELENS WORRY OLDHAM.

The Oldham Committee chose a somewhat experi- mental side for their home match with St. Helens, and it came near to defeat. The three-quarters proved weak, and a re-arrangement at the interval brought no better result. St. Helens were abort-banded during the second half through Heaton dislocating his shoulder, and yet they had most of the attack. Oldnara scored firs,, Farnsworth drawing most of the opposition before passing through Williams to Holbrook, who scored. Three minutes later Molyneux beat three opponents in midfield, and gave to Belsbaw with only Wood to challenge him, and the full back was beaten by a punt and follow up. Turtill added the goal. Wood, from a penalty, equalised before the interval. In the second nalf each side scored five points, Mavitty getting a try for the visitors,-and Jardine One for Oldham, Turtill improving one and Jardine the other. Result : Oldbam, two goals and two tries (10 points), St, Helens 2 goals and two tries (10 poinisl. WARRINGTON "A " V. RUNCORN " A.". —There was an attendatice of about 2,000 spectators to witnesS this match which was played at Wilderspool on Saturday. The teams were :—Warringtou, Brown; lwist, Hudson,

Moores and Davies ; Douglas. and Warren ; Naylor, Gibbs, Cooper, Valentine, Brandwood and Fletcher. Runcorn: falbot ; Whitfield, Fountain, Newell and Nicholls ; Murt and Hodgetts ; Rolliosun, Burke, Rogerson, Kirkham, Donoghue and Williams. A stiff breeze was blowing down the field and Runcorn had the advantage of it in the first half and at once assumed the aggressive. The Warrington defence was given a very hot time and Brown was very prominent in the earlier stages of the game. Warrington ought to have got over from a scrimmage but Davies sent the ball wide just when the centre had a clear opening. Douglas on ty just failed to get away on the right, but the ball was sent across to Davies who transferred it to Warren, who however was too well marked to do anything.Warrington were now playing a hard game but the visitors' defence was solid. Taloot received and punted to Davies, who, in trying to take the tall kick mulled it and the opposing pack were soon in a dangerous position and Burke got over for a good try. Undaunted, the Wirepullera pressed hard and Warren was responsible for some fine attempts but just before the :interval Nicholls out distanced the Warrington defenders and scored a beautiful try. The ensuing kick was badly placed and the iutt-r vest came with ths visitors ahead by two tries to nil.—Soon after the restart tbe homesters made good use of the advantageous breeze and toe forwards strove bard to equalize matters. Twist and Warren were VF ry con- epic uous but toe tricky little " half " was well marked and bad never a really good opening. Valentine was grounded when he bad every appearance of going nicely over. The visitors had undoubtedly the best of the second half for the Wirepullers seemed to be " fagged " after their fight against the stiff breeze in the first half. Play was very tame in the closing stages and the final came with Runcorn easy winners. Result : Runcorn, two tries (six points) ; Warrington, nil.

MANCHESTER ANTI DISIMICT LEAGUE. RYLANDS' RECREATION v. BARTON.

Rylands' Recreation opened their season on Saturday with a flee victory over Barton. At length Wallwork (the ex-Swinton player) scored a fine try for Barton. Early in the second half, Morris, by means, of a splendid try, enabled his side to take the lead, and Potter, who took the kick at goal, missed by only a few inches. Griffiths threaded his way through all opponents and running nearly the length of the field, got over, amid great enthusiasm, but Bramhall failed at goal. Play was very keen and three men were ordered from the ground. Rylands' men continued to hold the upper hand, and Potter taking a free kick well in landed a near goal. Result : Ryland!? WALTON V. MOORE AND DARESBURY:

An interesting game was played at Walton on Satur- day when Moore and Daresbury were the visitors. During the first half Walton had a slight breeze in their favour. The game was rather fast but nothing of note happened untiljafter 15 minutes play when a penalty kick was awarded to the visitors from which W de Prez scored a good goal. This seemed to liven things up a bit, but for some time the home defenders had a hot time of about 10 minutes, before the interval, a move- started by F Wilkinson ended in himself scoring a well earned goal, half-time arrived with no further results. Score : Moore and Daresbury, 2 goals ; Walton, nil. On resumption the teams seemed very evenly matched good half-back play was displayed by H Allen who seems ro be a half-back with good promise many good movements were promoted but very poorly executed. and no further score was made until almost on time when Clare scored for the home team. The whistle blew with the score Moore and Daresbury, 2 goals ; Walton, 1 goal. Among the spectators were Lady Greenall and her two sons, one of whom kicked-off for the first half and the other for the second half. __WORKINGMEN'S MISSION v. CHESHIRE LINES.

Workingmen's Mission did a good performance last Saturday by defeating Cheshire Lines A.F.C. by 5 goals to one. Any junior clubs in Warrington and district desirous of playing the Mission are requested to send for list of open dates to the Lion. Secretary, A. Boyle, 333, Welll3old-street, Warrington.

LATCHFORD V. STOCKTON BEATH.

At Latchford, before a fair crowd. For a long time neither side could claim any advantage, play being remarkably even. Smith, on the Latchford right, put on a nice run and centre,but Isherwood cleared Potter's final effort. The wind spoiled many promising move- ments by each side. At the interval there was no score. Play opened fast in the second half, each side putting in all they knew. For a long time the respective defences held their own. After about twenty minutes' play the Stockton Heath inside right put in a capital shot. which completely baffled the home custodian. The ball appeared to be passing out, but,suddenly swerving, found the net at the extreme corner. This was followed shortly after by an equalizer, Jackson scoring. Final ; Latchford, one goal ; Stockton Heath, one goal.

LYMM AMATEURS This match was played on Saturday on the ground of the Amateurs, before a fair crowd of spectators. Play had been in progress twelve minutes when Richardson scored a grand goal for the Amateurs. The Lads made strenuous efforts to get on equal terms with their opponents, but at this juncture the wind was stronger and helped the Amateurs to clear their lines, ond a raid was made on the Lads' defence, but they held out bravely, and 'redden, their custodian, gained the unstinted applause of the crowd for several grand clearances. At half-time the score was one goal against nil in favour of the Amateurs. On resuming the Amateurs were soon in difficulties and, owing to a miskick by. one of the backs, hind equalized. The Amateurs tried to make amends after this, but they met a stout defenceond, despite the good work of Millington and Hudson, they could not score. A raid to the other end resulted in s corner for the Lads, which was well placed, and, owing to faulty clearances by the Amateurs' goal-keeper, Barber scorcd an easy goal, which put the Lads in front. Toward the finish Saville was called upon three or four times, bnt he lie+ his goal intact, and the game ended in favour of Lrmm, with the score :—Lymm, two goals; Lymm Amateur, one goal.

NORTH CHESHIRE LEAGUE. SECOND DIVISION,. CADISHEAD WESLEYANS V. LATCHFORD RESERVE. These teams met at Cadishead on Saturday. This was the Wesleyans' first home game in the tournament. Mr Longdin was, said his Worship, really faithful and conscientious servant of the orporation, and a man of uodoubted ability, who had Performed h;s duties with such tact and judgment as to Zin the confidence and even admirition of all. (Hear, ear.) From first to last he retained the esteem and 2enfideoce of the Council and of all those with whom " Was associated. (Hear, hear.) Alderman TINNION, in seconding, said he had been Intimately connected with the late Surveyor aver since :le entered the Council, and he had always found him ‘1::' be a man of the highest possible integrity, a faithful ervant and very loyal to all his colleagues. Was exercised to the benefit of the town in Per:

When the minutes of the Finance and Estate Com- mittee were submitted for confirmation. Alderman BENNECT regretted that one item, the report by the elective and Mayor's auditors, appeared for the lest time. Henceforth the Council would be under the new regime. He knew that some members °proved that as being a desirable change, but he viewed it with the utmost possible regret. _ At a meeting of the Street Improvement Committee, a plan showing the widening and improvement of Winwick-road, near Longford Bridge, was submitted by the County Bridgemaster, and approved, and a letter from Messrs John White and Co., offering the land within the borough required for the proposed widening containing 1,683 square yards. for the sum of £lOO and costs was read, and it was resolved that the sum of £lOO be offered for the purchase of the land, which the company subsequently accepted subject to the payment of the legal and surveyor's costs at an agreed sum of £lO. This was also agreed to.

THE ILLNESS OF ALDERMAN JAMES EVANS. Alderman BURTON expressed the sympathy of the Council with Alderman James Evans in his illness, and voiced their satisfaction that the Chairman of the Health Committee was now on the highway to health again. The MAvou : lam glad that Alderman Evans is bidding fa .r to making a good recovery. (Hear, hear.)

PRIVATE STREETS. SANCTION TO LOAN OF £11,223.

Alderman TINNION intimated that the Local Govern- ment Board had forwarded their sanction to the borrow- ing of £11,223 for private street works. They had given their ssnctibn without any observations and were evidently perfectly satisfied with the manner in which the streets were to be constructed. At any rate they made no objections. ALDERMAN BOLTON'S REDEEMING FEATURE- Alderman BENNE•rr, in moving the adoption of the minutes of the Museum Committee, observed that the policy of the committee in opening dalivery stations in connection with the Library was meeting with success. Two further stations in Knutsford-road and in Marshhouse-lane had been opened, ministering to a long-felt want. They would be a convenience and a pleasure to a large number of inhabitants, lie also commented upon the fact that at the art, exhibition at Manchester two large sections were devoted to time works of Warrington artists— James Charles and George Sheffield—whose fame he noted with pleasure extenued tar beyond the confines of Warrington. Alderman Bolton .vas a donor to the exhibition. It was one of the redeeming features of Alderman Bolton that he had a kind of perverted love of art. (Laughter.) He also counted it to him for righteousness that he liked pictures and doubly counted it to him for righteousness that he allowed large numbers of others the privilege of viewing the works of art which he was able to acquire. (Laughter).

EVELYN-STREE r SCHOOL BATHS.

At a meeting of the Town Hall, Parks and Baths Committee the following resolution from the Sites and Buildings Committee of the Education Committee was read :—" That the Town Hall, Parks and Biths Com- mittee be informed that everything is now ready at the Evelyn-street Council School for the provision of the swimming bath and of the heating installation for the douche baths." It was resolved that the Surveyor be instructed to prepare plans and estimates with a view to application being made to the Local Government Board for sanction to the borrowing of the money. The minute was sanctioned by the Council. WHOLESALE PRODUCE MARKET.

At a meeting of the Markets Committee a letter from Mr Ernest Bennett with reference to the covering of the wholesale produce market, and asking for a reduc- tion in the tolls now charged owing to the alteration in the standard weigLt for the sale of potatoes, was read, and it was resolved that information be obtained from other towns as to tolls charged in their markets, and that the Borough Surveyor be inst.-noted to report upon the suggestion of covering over the wholesale produce market. This minute was approved. AUTOMATIC LAMPS.

Alderman JOHN EVANS, in moving the minutes of the Gas Committee,alluded to the adoption of a system of automatic lighting of lamps in by-streets. The lamps would be 1112 such a position that the lamplighters in tue main thoroughfares would be able to see whether the apparatus was in working order or not. MOTOR FIRE ENGINE FOR WARRINGTON.

The Fire Engine Committee baying discussed the advisability of purchasing a motor fire engine resolved :—" That in the opinion of this committee it is desirable to purchase a motor fire engine, and that the money standing to the credit of this committee in the Fire Engine Renewal Fund be appropriated for that purpose, the balance of the purchase money to be pro- vided for in the ensuing year's estimates ' : That a competent motor mechanic be appointed " ; "That the Deputy Borough Surveyor be instructed to make inquiries from local firms and report upon the retention of the services of an additional motor mechanic as an emergency man." The consideration of the further reorganisation of the brigade was deferred. Alderman BURTON, in moving the adoption of the minutes, thought the members would endorse the committee's decision.

Councillor ASHCROFT seconded the motion. Alderman BENNIUT cordially congratulated the com- mittee on tbeir decision. He recalled that the Chamber of Commerce passed a resolution on the subject and he then pointed out that the Chamber represented nearly all the principal manufacturers of Warrington. The decision would be a source of great satisfaction to them. The committee were doing the right thing, and he expressed his personal thanks and those of the Chamber of Commerce.

COOncinor HAWTHORN, "as one of those who grumbled at the last big fire," congratulated the com- mittee on the step they had taken. It bad been sug- gested that the time to be saved by the introduction of the motor-engine would be lost by the difficulty in getting the firemen on the scene any more promptly,but he hoped that would be obviated. The minutes were unanimously approved INCREASE OF SALARIES.

MR. MATHIAS, ELECTRICAL ENGINEER. The Electricity and Tramways Committee resolved that the salary of the electrical engineer (Mr. F. V. L. Mathias) be increased from .£lOO to £425 as and from the Ist October. TELFER, TRAMWAYS MANAGER. The Electricity and Tramways Committee resolved that the salary of the tramways manager (Ir. J. Telfer) be increased from £231 to £250 as and from the ISt October next. _

Both the increases were agreed to without discussion. The only comment was by Alderman SMETHURST. In moving the adoption of the minutes he said the com- mittee fully considered the matter and thought that after the excellent work which had been done during the past year or two the officials deserved the small iucreases.

Alderman BEN-NETT said it would be remembered that the Corporation obtained powers to run motor 'buses incertain outlying areas,and they ought to evercise those powers.Uniess they were prepared to extend the tram- way system itself they ought to coasider the out-lying districts which had not got the facilities possessed by the people in the centre of the town, in some other way. Personally he dad not like motor 'buses as they were ugly and apt to be dangerous unless properly managed—(" No, ho )—he was generalizing—but at the same time they would he a source of considerable convenience to the Inhabitants, and the 'buses might be run on a profit. The Bridges Committee the other day mct at Victoria Park and considered the direction of the approach roads to Howley Footbridge, and it was decided that the road should run from the foot of the embankment and follow the contour of the river until it touches the nearest fence of the Bowling Green, and then follow such fence to the main entrauce of the Park. It was also resolved that the tenders of Mr John Wood for the provision of steps at £3G 15a be accepted, and that the tender of the Richmond Gas Stove and Meter Co., Litd., for the casting of a bronze plate to commemo- rate the opening of the bridge, in accordance with the design submitted, at a cost of tO 10s, be accepted.

Alderman SmETHURST said that with regard to the request to construct a better approach to the bridge on the Howley side, he had made the attempt hut had failed. Alderman BOLTON Not the only man who is sorry for that. (Laughter). WARRINGTON BRIDGE. _ ANCIENT ORDER OF HIBERNIANS.

A social gathering was held under the auspices of the Ladies' Auxiliary Division of the Ancient Order of Hibernians at the C.T.L. rooms, Winwick-street on Thursday evening of last week. The occasion was the departure of the lady secretary, Sister M Cocoran, to take up a position on the teaching staff at Denny, Scotland. The chair was occupied by the President (Sister M Kilgalleon), and there were present : Sister Conroy, vice-president ; Sister Kelly, treasurer ; Sister M Currie arid Sister Percival, secretaries ; also Bros. I) Currie (president of the male division and county treasurers) ; E I) Crowley (Manchester County secretary) ; E Kelly (treasurer), E L Riley (secretary) and Councillor Owen Toole. During the interval in an excellent musical pro- gramme, the President spoke of the feeling of regret that the Division bad in losing the services of their' guest, Miss Corcoran, and called upon the Vice- President ti make a presentation of a handsome bangle, subscribed for by the Hibernians of Warring- ton.— Sister Conway, in making the presentation, spoke of the esteem and value in which Miss Corcoran's services were held by the Division.— Bro. Crowley, on behalf of the County Board, also paid a tribute to the work of tt4e retiring secretary, and conveyed the good wishes of the Order in Lancashire in her new sphere. Bro. Crowley voiced the gratification of the Board at the exceedingly large membership of that Division so recently formed and referred to the practical services that lady Hibernians were engaged upon in various centres. Bro. Currie, on behalf of the Male Division, eulogised the good work accomplished by Sister Corcoran, and referred to the satisfactory results, especially in connection with the Insurance Act section of the Order. He wished her all success, , Oosperily and happiness Sister Cocorau, in respond- ing, expressed her gratitude for the honour and appreciation paid her, and spoke of the pleasure with which she had conducted hsr duties for the Order, which she regretted to relinquish. The musical pro- gramme continued, and at the conclusion a vote of thanks to the President was accorded upon the proposition of Counciller Toole. The duties of M.C. were admirably performed by Bro. D. Currie.—The fortnightly meeting of the Branch was held on Sunday, Bro. D Currie, D P. presiding, and supported by Bros. E Handley, V.P., E L Riley (secretary), and Bro. E Kelly (treasurer)—Correspondence was read from Bro J D Nugent and from Bro. McLarney (Provincial Secretary) re the National Insurance Act ; also from Dr J D Liston resigning his position, tu accordance with the decision of the British Medical Association.—Bro. T Griffin also resigned his position as assistant secretary. Dr Liston's resignation was accepted with regret. Bro. Griffin's letter was left over to next meeting.—On the motion of Broa. J. Ryan and E. Kelley, a collection was taken for the Belfast workmen who have been thrown out of employment through the effects of the incendiary speeches of the Union leaders.—lt was also agreed to hold a concert in the N.S.L. Rooms on Thursday the 10th October for the purpose of further increasing the amount collected. Bro. J Ryan reported that satisfactory progress was being made regarding the proposed A 0.11. excursion to Dublin in June 1013. Over 70 ladies and gentlemen had given their names, and he felt confident the first excursion of the two divisions would be a huge success. The President announced that Sunday next was the Communion day , for both divisions, and he hoped there would be al record turnout at Sacred Heart Church that miming. He (the chairman) also referred to the coming visit of their late Chaplain, Father Dowley, from Waterford, who is to preach at Sacred Heart Church.

The death occurred at Willington, Bedfordshire, on Monday, of the Rev. Augustus Orlebar. vicar of the perish, and a prominent figure in "Tom Brown's Schooldays." It was he who, in the memorable match match against Marylebone described in that book, caught out the strong batsman and thus turned the game in Rugby's favour. In the "Schooldays" he was the original of the new boy Arthur, and, according to his own statement, was also the original Tom Brown in the fight with Slogger Williams.

ST. BENEDICTS SCHOOL.

COUNCILLOR ROBERTS OPENS NEW BUILDINGS.

The somewhat antiquated St. Benedicts School in Orford-lane, of a few months ago, has given place to a practically new and perfectly modern structure. Such a drastic change has come about owing to the old premises being far behind the requirements of the education authorities and the alterations have necessitated an expenditure of £2,500. It is needless to say that the present building is a vast improve- ment, and the school managers along with those who hays supported the venture are deserving of congratulation, not only from the local education authority but from the inhabitants of Orford-lane and the immediate district whose children are receiving their education at this up-to-date school. The new school, which was designed by Mr H Honan, of Liverpool, will accommodate 600 children. Although this number is not such a great increase, the space allotted to each child is considerably greater, and therefore the health of the scholars is better safe- guarded. There was a fair attendance at the opening cere- mony which was performed by Councillor H Roberta (chairman of the Education Committee), on Monday, when the Rev T 0 Swarbrick presided. He was supported by Councillor and Mrs Roberts, Councillor Henshall, the Revs It A Dawson, B Primavesi B Mawson, P L Huggins and Wilson (Latchford), Mr j Murray (director of education), Mr B H Roberts (assistant director of education), Mr D Morris (head- master), the members of the teaching, staff, and Mr McKinley, Mr Vernon. A number of residents in the neighbourhood were also present. The CHAIRMAN said teat was the first opportunity they had bad since the alterations had been completed of having the formal ouening ceremony. He was anxious that the representatives of the Education Corn-. mittee and the officials of the town should see what they had done in order to comply with the iequirements of the Education Committee to bring the school up to the demands which were made upon them for increased accommodation, lighting and ventilation. The old systems were considered absolutely inadequate, but now they had rectified their omissions and made the school all right. (Hear, hear.) By the aid of their architect they had transformed the school from an antiquated building into quite an up-to-date structure, and it was as efficient as almost any other school in tcrwn. (Applause.) That showed that the congrega- tion knew that whatever the cost might be and what- ever the sacrifice was, they were determined to have their schools as efficient as possible. The schools were not quite complete as regarded decoration' but considering the short time at their disposal and the large amount of work to be done, the Education Committee should feel quite satisfied. that they had only lost a fortnight of school time, thatlbeing due to the energies of the architect and the builders. The alterations would be a great advantage to both teachers and scholars, but such a change could not be made without great expense and sacrifice. He hoped they would have the sympathy of all the people in the town who were interested in education and especially denominational education. (Applause). Councillor ROBERTS, who was accorded a hearty reception, said it afforded him sincere pleasure and great personal gratification to be present and take part in the opening of that very useful and beautiful building. (Applause.) He had a slight recollection of the old school. It was a comparatively small building ,and, as they would think now-a-days, very insufficiently adapted for carrying on the work of education. They had made great changes and when one remembered the short time taken to carry out those great alterations 0129 imagined that Father Swarbrick and those associated with him must have possessed something in the nature of the magician's wand. (Laughter.) He could say without reserve th t the Education Committee of the town and the cause of education generally owed a debt of gratitude to those who had brought about that great and substantial change. It was a public debt and one which he, as Chairman of the Education Committee, had great pleasure in acknowledging. (Applause.) He was also ple4sed to pay public testimony to the value as a colleague on their Education Committee of the Rev Father Wilson, who had gained the respect and appreciation of all with whom be came into contact. (Applause.) Father Wilson had no warmer admirer than himself in regard to the thoroughness and self- sacrifice which he continually displayed in his attention to educational matters, and he was extremely sorry that his indisposition prevented him being there that day. (Hear, hear). The number of scholars they had to deal with at the present time in the town was no less than 13,585. Of that dumber no less than 9,318 were children who belonged to the voluntary schools and 4,267 to the Council schools. Of the 9,318 no fewer than 2,500 belonged to that Church. Their denomination had provided accommodation for 2,241, so that they were exceeding their accommodation by the number of children they had to provide for to a slight extent. When, however, they had carried out what he under- stood was their intention, to provide a separate boys school, they would hare fully complied with the requirements of their own particular denomination and would be in advance of the rest of the town, inasmuch as they would have provided accommodation to the full extent of their requirements. He wished them God speed in that direction. (Applause). The Education Committee also had their responsibilities and were behind the times far more than were their Catholic friends. They had to deal with 13 585 children, and had provided only 12,808 places. So thsy were responsible, as an Education Committee, for a considerable amount of overcrowding in the town. They had no fewer than four schools in prospect, which would probably take them about six years to complete. lie hoped that by that time the demands of the town would still exceed the supply, and that they would go on making the pro- vision necessary for the proper education of the children. He congratulated them on the appearance of the children. He knew that the parish was a poor one and it was all the more creditable to the denomination that none of the little ones went short in respect to He was glad to think that in this country, might be happening elsewhere, there was less education.whatev whatever

REV. S. F. COLLIER'S VISIT TO BOLD-STREET NEXT WEEK.

Great interest lass been awakened in local religions circles by the announcement that special nteetings on behalf of the social work of the world famed Manches- ter and Salford Mission, will be held in Bald-street Wesleyan Chapel, Warrington, on T hursday next, October 10th, and that the Rev. S. F. Collier (President Designate of the Wesle3ao Methodist Conference) and "Gipsy" Smith, the famous evangelist, will be the chief speakers. Two Mission " Sisters " will also form part. of the deputation. The special appeal of these meetings to local residents lies in the fact that the rescue, relief, and preventive work of the Manchester AI ission is entirely unsectarian and that the effort is widespread in its scope and extent. The headquarters of the Mission are at the Central Hall, Manchester, with seven other centres in the neediest parts of Manchester and Salford; and -rith a complete equipment of Homes, Refuges, and Labour Yards which prove most helpful adjuncts to the wore. 'The Men's Home Refuge, and Labour Yard: a splen- did building erected at crest cost and fitted up for the express purpose of dealing with homeless and destitute men, hay S5O beds. Here workless, hungry and friend- less, hungry, and ftiendlesi men are received every morning. In the winter months, as many as 150 poor fellows have been taken in daily; and provided with work, shelter and food. Everything possible is done to enable a man to retain his self-respect under adverse circumstances: and to feel that it is not " charity," but opportanity, the home provides. One distinct advantage of the system in vogue at the Mission Homo is that the casual workers are allowed out early in the morning to seek other employment whereas, under the poor law rule of the Casual Ward the men are debarred from making application until late in the day. A Labour Bureau in connection with the home does effective work in securing permanent and temporary jobs for deserving men. In addition to the Men's Home, Refuge and Labour Yards, there is also a Women's Hume, shelter with workrooms, where, day and night, friendless and homeless women and girls are received and cared for. Another most commend- able mission centre is the Preventive Home for Young Girls, situated at Reddish, near Stockport, where children who have shown evil tendencies, or who are living in perilous suroundings, are taken in, kept by the Mission, and trained by the walkers for domestic service. A maternity home and hospital where young women who have been tempted and have fallen are received, nursed aid tended through confinement, end influenced toward a better life, is another valuable adjunct of this Mission ; and the social and moral worth of such an agency can be estimated by the fact that no fewer than 95 per cent of the young women thus helped have turned out well. In many cases, not. only has the life of mother and babe been safe-guarded, but the homes from which the young mothers come have also been saved from trouble and disr race. Over 400 men, women and girls from all parts of the country are thus regularly housed by the Mission, and at least ISO of these are destitute, friendless, or home- less when they enter the Home, while many of them are also degraded. In addition to this work there is also a well-oraanised system of relief. More than once in times of special distress the municipal authorities have sought the advice and help of the Mission, and have afterwards commended very highly the way in which the relief has been carried out. The Rev. S. F. Collier, who has superintended the work of the Manchest.r Mission from its inception in is one of the best known social workers in the country. At the Wesleyan Conference held in Liver- pool this year Mr. Collier, by is large vote, was nominated to the Presidency of the Connexion for 1913. Gipsy Smith's fame as an eYarigelist is world-wide. He has hut recently returned from another visit to America where, in the far Western States, he has been holding special missions with remarkable results, preaching to audiences that crowd the largest halls. In many places the weer-night congreations numbered eight thousand people..

'Lae fact that not only are many applications for relief, assistance, and advice received by the Mission workers from this district year by year, but that. Christian workers throughout the country (including Police Court missionaries) are ccnstantly making appeals to the Mission to take elm they have in blind, and for which they have no adequate pi °vision in their own town or village, is a weigny ground of appeal to local sympathy and support. And the claim of the late Rev. Dr. McLaren that "No finer bit of work for Christ and man is being done in England to-day "is surely a magnificent testimony to the value of the work for which the appeal is made.

THE REVISING BARRISTER'S DIGNITY.

The party agents at the Runcorn Revision Court, ort Monday,addressed the l'arrister as " Mr Williams." " My name," said the barrister severely, "is not Williams, but Colt,Willianaa, and the proper way to address me is Sir 'or ' Your honour.' I object to be. called by name. lam tired of being addressed as Mr Williams."

So it is probable 'that this " model " tariff will appear, with Mr. Bonar Law's imprimatur, in time to be of use to the high Protectionist party in the United States Presidential election. Last 'week President Taft described a private con- ference of British Motor manufacturers called together by a London Protectionist news- paper as an indication that "While Demo- crats think Protection is not good for America, England is beginning to see that 'she must adopt it," although the main effect of the agitation got up by the London paper ;was to show what immense strides the motor Industry in this country has made and can make without Protection. The local Insurance Committee has com- pleted arrangements with the Corporation for the use of Sankey Sanatorium for the treatment of consumptive Patients under the provisions of the National Insurance Act. Warrington is thus one of the first towns in the country to work the Act in its entirety. Dr. Hibbert, Medical Officer of Health, has been appointed superintendent of the Sanatorium. The first patients under the Insurance Act have been admitted this week. * *

In connection with the unemployment. section of the National Insurance Act, Courts of Referees will shortly be constituted by the Board of Trade to deal with questions arising on workmen's claims to unemployment benefit in the Warrington district. The workmen's representatives on this Court will be drawn from a panel elected by ballot' of the workmen in the insured trades. Nominations for this election should be made as soon as possible, and in any case not later than Saturday, 19th October. For the purpose of the election electoral wards will be constituted, and a separate election will take place in each ward :—Warrington and Lymm have been constituted one ward, and will be entitled to return five repre- sentatives, allocated to the follow- ing trades :-2 to building and construction of works • 3 to shipbuilding, engineering and ironfounding, construction of vehicles and sawmilling. Any 25 workmen who have had unemployment books issued to them before 15th September from any local office within a ward, and who, in the case where al candidate is nominated for a specified trade group, themselves belong to such trade group can nominate a candidate for such ward or group. Nomination papers for each wardl and full information as to the election and duties of Referees will be obtainable on and after 28th September, 1912, at the •Old Post Office (Labour Exchange) Warrington, and at Mr. J. F. Niddrie's, the Cross, Lymm.

Heady Cock, MA, (afternoon), and the Vicar (the Rev. W. Bracecamp) in the evening. Warrington Man Killed at Leigh.

William Howard, aged about fifty years, a hawker of pots, residing at 22, Queen-street, Wairington, was killed on Wednesday night at the West Leigh Station of the London and-North-Western Railway Company about five minutes past seven. So far as can' be ascertained, Howard opened the carriage dcor before the train stopped, stumbled, held the handle of the door for the moment, and then slipped under the train and was killed. He was badly crushed on the head, and his arm broken.—Death was almost instantaneous. He leaves a widow and family.

KITCHEN RANGES, Fire Grates and Tiles, Large stock. Inspection invited.—J. S. GARLAND, 51-55, Bridge et., Warrington.—Advt. Warrington Pianist's Success. We are pleased to announce that Mr. T. W. Pearce, the well-known Warrington pianist, has been awarded the Halle scholarship which entitles him to three years' free tuition at the Royal Manchester Royal College of Music. Mr. Pearce is already a student at the College, and a pupil of Mr. Max Mayer—one of the fortunate few who studied with the great pianist and composer- Franz Lizst. Throughout the higher 1 branches of the profession, Mr. Mayer is recognised as one of the leading teachers of the pianoforte, and the last holder of the Hall 6 scholarship was a pupil of his. The scholarship was founded by the subscribers to the Halle Memorial Fund, in commemoration of Sir Chas. Halle, first principal of the College. It is awarded trienally, and is open to all College students. Mr. Pearce is to be heartily congratulated on gaining this much coveted distinction, and his many Warring- ton friends will wish him well in his musical career.

The Examiner, Saturday, October 5,1912.

ASYLUM TRAGEDY.

ATTENDANT AND PATIENT DROWNED

AT WINWICK.

A senaational double tragedy occurred at the County Asylum at Winwick yesterday afternoon, an attendant and a patient being drowned in a disused pit. Between three and four o'clock a party of male patients was out for exercise in the grounds, in charge of several attendants. When passing a disused brick pit on the estate, one of the inmates named Edward Benyon, suddenly broke away from the walking party and swiftly climbing over the fence surrounding the pit, jumped into the water. An attendant named Louis Thomas, of Chester, pluckily went to rescue the man although he could not swim. He reached the drowning patient, who clutched at him wildly. A desperate struggle ensued and both men sank. Several of the other attendants jumped into the water, but it was so icy cold that they could not iemain in for more than two or three minutes. Further assistance was quickly forthcoming, and the chief clerk at the Asylum lMr. F. Uncles) subse- quently recovered the two bodies. Artificial respira- tion was tried for over an hour, but it was of no avail.

_ Bicycle Thief. A youth named Samuel Marsh, of no settled abode, was charged at the Warrington Borough Court, yesterday, with stealing a gents' bicycle value £8 ss, the property of William Dudd, 0, Matthew-street The prisoner, who was a brother-in-law of the prosecutor, staved at the latter's house on the night of Stptember 29. Early next morning he was seen by Mrs. Matthews wheeling the bicycle out of the house. He never returned. The police from inquiries ascertained that he was connected with a horse stealing 'ca.*: A remand of a week was necessary in order that be could be committed to the Liverpool Sessions and ,then sent for a term under the 80l stall system. Wesleyan Cycling Club. The last run of the season took place on Saturday. Assembling at Victoria-square the eight members who formed the party ran by the main road intending to make Chester their objection. The following wind made the ride very pleasant and easy. When Cornhill Farm at Dunham was reached they dismounted and enjoyed a very well served tea. After tea, blackberry picking turned their thoughts away from Chester, and the remainder nUthe journey was abandoned. As the wind had somewhat abated a quick return was made through Alvanley and Delatriere Forest. On Thursday evening the annual social was held at the Winmarleigh Cafe. Seventy-five members and friends took part in the games, interspersed with musical and other pieces. The most amusement was occasioned by the game dubbed by its inver,tors " Rear Lights,' Miss Jones and Mr. Sinclair being declared the winners. Mr. A. Dutton gave an excellent rendering of two favourite recitations, "Gongs Deen " and " Holloa." Miss Mc:Bton delighted the company with her recitation " The Telegram," and Miss Ward, Miss Kershaw, and the quartet party supplied vocal items, the solos given being" Home dearie home," and "Should they ask you what's your name " respectively. The catering was as usual excellent.

Liverpool Cadet Battalion C L B. Captain H. T. Cotton, of Orford Barracks, inspected Warrington's cadet battalion on Sunday in the grounds of The Honks, Latchford, which were very kindly placed at the disposal of the battalion by Mrs. Broad- bent. Lieutenant-Colonel Appleton was in command of the parade, and was assisted by Major J. H. Brookes and Adjutant I. Sankey. Colonel D. Backton (hon. colonel of the battalion) was also present. The proceedings commenced with a drumhead service the choir boys from Christ Church. Latchford, being in attendance, and as they paraded round the ranks singing" Onward, Christian Soldiers," were preceded by four drummers and followed by the Rev W Brace- camp (assistant battalion chaplain) and the Rev G C Briggs (chaplain Christ Church Company) who had four colour-sergeants as a bodyguard. The inspecting officer was afterwards received with the " general salute," and following a vigorous individual inspection the battalion marched past in column and quarter column, Captain Cotton having in the meantime taken up his position at the saluting base. Company move- ments were next proceeded with, and these seemed to impress the cfficer very much. At the close he expressed himself as delighted with what he had seen. The Hon. Colonel then :presented the Cadet Commissions to the officers. Major Brooks presented a silver spoon bearing the C.L.B. badge and a suitable inscription to Mrs.Broadbent on behalf of the battalion for her kindness in allowing them the use of the grounds. Mrs. Broadbent responded. Order of Shepherds. Under the auspices of the Loyal Order of Ancient Shepherds (A.U.) MR. ENOCH SANKEY'S AFFAIRS. BETTING AND BANKRUPTCY.

Mr. Enoch'William S inkey, of Heath Farm, Croft, formerly one of the best known horse dowers in Lancashire, appeared at the Warrington Bankruptcy Court yesterday, before Mr Registrar Rldg way, for his adjourned public examination in bankruptcy. Mr J Grant Gibson (official receiver),conducted the examina- tion ; Mr T E Knowles represented Messrs Boydell Bros., the petitioning creditors; and Mr Amos (Messrs Robert Davies and Co.), appeared tor the debtor. The examination had beer) adjourned for the produc- tion of certain accounts which had now been Sled.

The Official Receiver raid the accounts produced showed that on January let, 1911, the deficiency amounted to £8,326, and on November 15th, when the deed of assignment was executed, it had risen to £11,729 15s Bd. Ihe increase was mainly due to the reduction of farming stock and the complete elimina- tion of book debts' which cauaed the assets to shrink from £3,705 to £1,148. Debtor was questioned by the Official Receiver as to the amount of money he had "got through," apart from trade receipts and payments. He admitted that when quite a boy he received £5OO from his mother, that on his coming of age he was paid £l,OOO from his father's estate, that be realized £6,500 on the sale of an hotel at Croft, and £B,BOO on the sale of New Hey Farm. The Official Receiver : The total of those figures is £16,800, and if to that is added L 11,729, the deficiency at the date of the deed of assignment, we get a grand total of £28,529. Trutt represents the amount of money that you have lost or spent, doesn't it ? The Debtor : That is right, sir. What, shortly, is the explanation for the loss of a fortune and the incurring ot surplus liabilities approach- ing £12,000 ?—General expenses, I think. If I were to seek to express it in one cause, would I he far wrong if I were to say " extravagant living "? It was, to some extent. To use the common expression, you have lived well ?—I have lived well. That is right. Would you be able to tell us what your position was at any time during your business career ?—I could not really tell yon. Shall I be putting it unfairly if I say you always bad plenty of money in your bands ?—I always thought I was all tight. You were never pinched for money ?—No, I didn't bother about it. Didn't the fact that you had to raise a considerable sum on mortgage indicate to you that you were not quite in such easy circumstances as the mere passing of money might lead you to think ?-oh, no. I always had a mortgsge. Debtor was closely questioned as to loans which be obtained from Mr Bardsley of the Railway Hotel, Demsgate, Manchester. He denied that one suos Ot £lOO was tor a wedding present for his daughter. Mr Knowles then questioned the debtor. You told us at your previous examination that prior to the deed of assignment you had done plenty of gambling. "Plenty "is the word you used. Do you remember that ?-1 forget. Can you give us any idea of the sums you have won and lost in gambling ?-1 cannot tell you. Have you no means of a.certaining?—None whatever. Haven't you kept the slightest record ?—No. Then it may be that you have been dealing in thousands of pounds, for gambling purposes ?—I shotnd not think it very likely. Still it may be so ?—lmpossihle. Why do you say impossible ?-Ilow could I gamble thousands of pounds. ? I don't mean to say £l,OOO at one bet, but taking gambling as a whole ?-013, I dare say I have had £l,OOO of bets in my time. You eould not go beyond that ?—I may have had £2.000. I could not tell you. I have no record at all. What has been the average amount of your bets ?—I have had a" liver ". on a horse sometimes. That is about the limit is it ?—No, it is not the limit. If I thought it was a good thing, I should have a" termer "on it probably. Debtor was questioned with regard to two cheques for between £7OO and £BOO which he showed to Messrs Boydell, Bros. His explanation was that, on behalf of a friend, be backed an " outsider " at the Grand National at 40 to 1. If the horse won he was to receive £BOO sled if it lost the friend would have to pay £25. The horse won, but the man with whom the bet was made proved unreliable. His cheques, which debter showed t 3 Messrs Boydell Bros., were worthless and were ultimately torn up by debtor and his friend. Debtor denied that his motive:in showing the cheques was to create a false impression. He was not trying to get any further credit and he was merely show jug the cheques to indicate that he . had accounts which he could not collect. Mr Knowles : Faye you been earning any money during the last three months ?—No. You have travelled about very considerably, haven't you ?—I have. Just about the same as you did before ?--No, not the isame. Ydu bave been abroad, haven't you ?—No, I haven't been abroad. You have been to the South of France. haven't you ? •—No. I have been to the North of France. I have , been on the Continent. _ - -

Who has paid your expenses ?—Friends. With regard to the motor car, I suggest von h.ven't told us everything about that remarkable bat ?—I told you everything about it. NVasn't there a sum of OW connected with that bet? —I could not tell you. I have forgotten all about the motor car.

I suggest that the bet you told us of is a most unlikely one—that a man should wager whether he should retain his own motor car or not ?—I don't suggeit that at all. I should toss him whether I gave him so much or nothing for it. That would be the arrangement of the business. In answer to Mr Amos, Mr Sankey said it was not the custom of horse dealers to keep elaborate accounts. His business had always been profitable but of late years it hsd been seriously injured by the adoption of motor traction. He did not bet as a habit and he did not think he bad lost money by it, on the whole. It was necessary to bet toe small extent in his business. He would not hare done such a foolish thing as to bet £3OO to a motor car. The deficiency of £12,000 came as a great surprise to him; he hal had no idea be owed anything like that amount. He was anxious to avoid bankruptcy proceedings at all costs as some of his larg sat trading connections had advised him to do so. He denied that be had been recklessly extravagant. Asked to give a definition of the extravagance which he had previously admitted, debtor said : Living well at home and entertaining customers. Mr Amos: It was the natural sequence of the conduct of your business ?—That is right. Are you worth anything to-day ?—Nothing. You have parted with all your estate ?—Everything. Regarding the local creditors, have you any intention towards them ?-1. have. What is that intention ?—To pay them at the first Opportunity. Having regard to your personal position, have you any expression of regret te, make ?—Only for myself. Not for the creditors ?—I am sorry for the creditors certainly. The examination was closed. •

AMONG THE SPIRITUALISTS.

" Surprise Party and Presentations. • On Tuesday evening the Druids' Hall, Sankey-street, Warrington, was crowded with a very happy throng,the occasion being what was termed a " surprise parry," which certainly did not belie its name. The room had been tastefully decorated with flags, flowers, streamers, ,to., and conspicuously placed over the platform was a portrait of the President (Mr W E Stout), and,it being that gentleman's birthday, the motto " Many happy returns to Pa," as be is familiarly called. Mr .1 Fulton Smith presided, and in his opening remarks said he was glad to be with them as he believed their object was the good of humanity. A capital entertainment was then proceeded with, Mrs. Speed who was the accompanist throughout the evening, opening with a pianotorte solo "Home, sweet home.' Maggie Handles followed with a humorous recitation, " My broken-hearted D311y," for which she as loudly applauded. The Misses Foster and Kenwright skilfully performed two mandoline duets, and two comical trios, in character, were given by Messrs. Osborne and Proverbs end a friend. Later in the evening Mr W Proverbs played a mazurka in good style on the violin. Miss Annie Linughton sang very nicely "The garden of roses" and "Meet me to-night in Dreamland." Miss Eva Tanner,the possessor of a sweet soprano voice,delighted the audience with her, rendering of "In sunny Spain" and "Orange blossom." Mr J E Hannan was loudly encored for his singing of "Good company," responding with another song. Miss Ashley recited with good expression the dialogue between Brutus and Cassius, and Mr Alf Dutton's recitation from Kipling was given with such effect as to secure an encore, to which he responded with " Elalloa!". "Be British," a song and recitation describing the lose of the Titanic. was effectively given by Mr W Gilbert, aided by a number of Lyceum girls as la chorus. A garland dance was given by Elsie Mellor and Dolly Handles, and following a cornet solo by Mr Shelmerdine, this part of the programme was closed with a sketch entitled " The automatic laundry," by the Lyceum children, which provoked roars of laughter. At this stage of the evening refreshments were handed round, following which the " surprise " portion of the programme was unfolded. As has been previously intimated, it was the President's birthday, and the Chairman, after congratulating Mr Stout on that fact, and tr,shing him still many years of life to continue his work iulthe cause which he has at heart, presented him, on behalf of the subscribers, with an enlarged photo- graph of his deceased wife and himself, also with a handsome silver-mounted "Globetrotter" folding walk- ing cane, with engraved initials. The Secretary ;Mr C Nal:issuer) was the next to be "surprised" by Mrs W H Sherburn coming forward and presenting to him an enlarged photograph of himself and Mrs. Manssuer as a slight acknowledgment of their self-sacrificing work for the benefit of the cause. Mrs Nightingale added to the surprises by presenting to the President.

for the tee of the Society, two handsome wooden , offertory plates.
