LOCAL PARLIAMENT.

DEBATE ON THE KING'S SPEECH.

Tories _ and the Insurance Act.

Our local Parliament, in other words, the War- rington Parliamentary Debating Society, is once more shaping the affairs of the nation. Another session's work was inaugurated in promising style on Thursday evening with the debate on the King's Speech. The large attendance in- dicated a marked revival of interest in the society's doings, all sections of the House being well filled. This session the large room at Cairo-street schools has been taken, and ample accommodation is thus afforded for members and visitors. A Conserva- tive Ministry is in office, and on Thursday there was quite a galaxy of talent on the Front Bench, no fewer than three prospective Parliamentary candidates attending to give the Government their support. Mr. J. Ball was in the Speaker's chair, and, before the debate began, returned thanks to the House for his re-election.

----- ----- The Ring's Speech forecasts measures dealing with : - -

• 1. Repeal of th 6 Parliament Act and the Re- form of the House of Lords. 2. Amendment of the Insuratice Act. 3. Industrial Unrest.

4. Imperial Defence. 5. Fiscal Reform. 6. Housing Reform. 7. Poor Law Reform. 8. Imperial Preference. 9. Social Reform. 10. Imperial Federation.

11. Electoral Reform.

The President of the Board of Trade (Mr. P. W. Devonport) moved the address of thanks to the Throne. He viewed with apparent horror the proceedings of a House of Commons which, un- controlled, could put into force any -fantastic" legislation it liked. The position of the Liberal Government, which wile compelled to "toe the lii.e" by its friends, was a degradation of poli- tics, and a discredit to our Porliamentary institu- tions.

The First Lord of the Admiralty (Mr. R. L. Briscoe) seconded, and laid down, as one of the chief aims of the Unionist Government, the rais- ing of the standard of living. The method, of course was the erection of Protective tariffs.

The Leader f the Opposition (Mr. .T. darman) was sceptical as to the profuse promises of the Government. The King's Speech, he said, con- tained six promises of reform. If the Government had added a seventh—reform of the Tory Party— they would have reached perfection—(laughter),-- but until they did so. he was afraid none of the other reforms would be worth the paper it Was printed on. The Government was in too great a hurry to repeal the Parliament Act and reform the House of Lords. There was no sign that the House of Lords itself was anxious for reform. The wisest plan would he to keep the Parliament Act in force until they had formulated their Bill for the reform of the House of Lords, because they might need it to get the House of Lords to accept their Bill. (Applause.) Liberals had always felt that it was no use talking about refdrni of the House of Lords, so long as there was no means of compelling that House to accept it. As to the Insurance Act, the Leader of the Opposition twit- ted the Government on its unwillingness to re- peal a measure which they had represented as so horrible. He was 'rather doubtful of a promise of housing reform which came from the landowning classes. What was behind it all? Did it mean that they wanted to sell their land or to increase their ground rents? As to elec- toral reform, he understood the Government, did not intend ,to include the enfranchisement of women, although the late Leader of the Govern- ment was strongly in favour of it. Mr. Harold Smith, M.P., had declared in that House that he did not believe in plural voting, but would the Tory Government he so bold as to abolish it? He was afraid that what might be expected was only another edition of a property qualification, which would perhaps keep the Workingman even farther away from the possibilities of a vote. Mr. Greaves Lord (prospective Tory candidate for Ince) questioned the Leader of the Opposition's assertion that the House of Lords had showil no desire for reform. Had he forgotten that a former leader of his own party—Lord Rosebery—had within the last four years succeeded in passing through the House of Lords a series of resolutions in favour of reform?

The Leader of the Onposition t Resolutions !

Mr. Greaves Lord : Resolutions of the House of Lords had just as much force as resolutions of the House of Cornmons, and are not ouite as silly. The Leader, of the Opposition : But they are not Acts.

Mr. Greaves Lord admitted that there had been times when the Second Chamber had not quite done its duty—nobody on the Conservative side would dream of saying that the House, as at pre- sent constituted, had been perfect in its actions. What, they did say, however, was that on many occasions the House of Lords had stood by the people against the extravagant demands of a Liberal oligarchy, or of the Crown. He further claimed that on every occasion when it had been clearly and accurately determined what the will of the people was, the House of Lords had given adequate expression to it. If there was one thing that would make a reformed House of Lords a, weakness to the country. it was the entire aboli- tion of the hereditary principle. Having con- demned the fashion of talking about the rights of democracy without emphasising responsibility, Mr. Lord said if there was one thing that made one almost disgusted with party politics to-day, it was that, owing to the extensions of the fran- chise and to the fact that those to whom the franchise was extended, were very often not fit to exercise it—they had both political parties en- gaged in a shameful competition as to which could give the most rights and inflict the least responsi- bilities. _ _

Why should the country be ruled by men simply because they happened to be the first-born of a family, or had inherited the means of exploiting their fellow men and building up huge fortunes? In our schools, the children were drilled and dragooned, and taught to cheer at the sight of a flag which someone had christened a Union Jack, but which could not be interpreted to mean anything of any benefit to them. He hoped the Cohservative Government, in any Bill they brought forward, would at-least see that the recipients got value for their money. Discussing industrial unrest, Mr. Clarke said be would not interfere with the right of the worker to strike. (Hear, hear.) That was the only right he had got. (A Socialist : "That and work.") The interruptor referred to the right to work as being one of the monopolies of the Labour party, but from what he (the speaker) had seen of those who advocated the policy of Socialism and the prin- ciples of Labour, they were the last to get to it. A RECHABITE MAJORITY,

WARRINGTON JUVENILE DISTRICTS' COMING OF AGE.

' The inauguration of the Temperance Movement, was responsible for one of the greatest and best crusades against the pernicious system which existed of friendly societies' holding their meetings in public houses,aud it soon became manifest to the early 'Temperance workers that Friendly or thrift societies. ' must be sepqrated from the drink shop.ln 1835 a Total Abstinence Friendly Society was instituted in Salford and was named the Independent Order of techabites the name being suggested by the striking incident recorded in the 35th Chapter of Jeremiah. The Rechabite Order has always been, and still continues, in the van of progress and efficiency, as the increas- ing membership and accumulated funds plainly illustrate. Branches were formed in all parts, and in 1891 the Warrington Juvenile District No. 4 was formed, and since its inception has made rapid strides. The progress which has been made from the first has been under review during the week, for the society have been holding the coming-of-age celebrations. The Committee's report, which is included in a hand- some souvenir along with other interesting items, states that at the beginning of the year 1891 there were seven adult Tents with a membership of just ' over 300. As the adult sections were beginning to arouse themselves a little, they thought it was advisable to form a Juvenile District with a Distr:ct Funeral Fund, and with that object in view a committee was elected, and after holding several meetings the Wallington Juvenile District was duly instituted on September 28th, 1891, at Bryn near Wigan. The leport then deals fully with the pro- gress made by the society and states that since January, 1892, up to the present time 455 members had been transferred to the adult tents. They had also paid out in funeral claims on the deaths of 106 members £4OB 16s. and over 1,550 was fairly invested. The membership at the end of June 30th was 1,431 comprising 20 Juvenile Tents. The Juvenile District is worked apart from the Adult District and has its own officers. In conclusion the Committee sincerely hoped that all brethren and sisters would endeavour to do their best to push forward their Itechabits principles and never cease to work until every boy and girl had been secured t r membership in the ' Order.

The celebrations during the week have taken the form of public meetings and social evenings in the various parts of the district, and commenced with a huge demonstration at Asiaton-in-Makertleld on Saturday I last, followed by a poblio meeting in the Congrega- tional schools Meetings have also been held at Orrell, Havdock, Downall Green, Bryn, Lowton St. Mary's, and in 'Warrington at the Wycliffe Memorial Hall, Brick- street Mission Hall, West-street Mission Hall, Friar' Green schoolroom, Thew lis-street schoolroom, Wesleyan schoolroom, Stockton Heath, Wesleyan schoolroom, Padgate, and Wesleyan schoolroom, Penketh. The speakers have been Bros R Bell, S A Scarlett, J H Lear Caton, and A Tomlinson. Capital musical programmes have been submitted at all the meetings and the celebrations which terminate to-day, have been a huge success. MEETING IN WYCLIFFE MEMORIAL HALL. There was a fair attendance in the Wycliffe Memorial Hall, Warrington, on Wednesday evening when an enthusiastic meeting was held. Mr J Upson presided and the speaker was Bro A Tomlinson. The banner which the Wycliffe Tent 2214 received for securing the highest number of new members during the nine months ending June was placed on the platform. The CHAIRMAN was pleased to have a share in those perfectly justifiable rejoicings by which they were celebrating the attainment of the majority in the Juvenile Tents of the district. It was very satisfactory Ito know that the Juvenile Tents had made such rapid progress in the comparatively short period of 21 years. They had now passed through the penniless ages of infancy, boyhood, and adolescence, and the district had now, reached the age of energetic manhood and that night they were celebrating the fact. It spoke well for the energy,self denial and sacrifice and skill of the pioneers of the movement and those who stood by the juvenile tents in their penniless period, infancy and youth. He thought that whilst they were celebrating the attainment of their majority and taking a retrospect of the past, whilst looking back on the way they had come, and rejoicing in the achievements they had made, at the same time he thought they ought to take a prospective view of the position. He was quite sure the officers in that district did not intend to rest on the laurels they had won but that they would look on the success they had achieved and take from that a stimulus for the future. It was no small achievement to have gained 1434 during the time the district had existed. There was however, an immense field before them which was at present untouched. (Hear, hear.) He thought be was well within the limit when he said that there were 20,000 children in the county borough of Warrington who were eligible for the Juvenile Tents. Probably they would not be able to get the majority of those children in, but he thought they ought to aim at getting 5,000 or 0,000 of the children of Warrington in their tents. That might seem a large ideal, but large ideals would only bring about large achievements. (Hear, hear ) Dealing with the need to get the children in the Rechabite order as soon as ever possible, the speaker said it was a far easier matter to prevent youths from falling away than it was to rescue those who had fallen. They wanted to instil in the hearts and minds of the younger generation the ideals of thrift and temperance, and then they would be well on the way to securing respectable citizenship They wanted to push forward their good principles so that it would be made possible for the children who were living in darkness to come out of the gloom and evil, and find their way into the light of freedom and happiness. (Applause). They deserved every encouragement in their noble cause, end these celebrations would give them added stimulus to)so on to greater accomplishments. 'Applause). Bro. IL BELL (Middlesborough) then gave an interesting and instructive address on the objects of the movement and dealt with their position as an approved society under the Insurance Act. A musical programme was ably disposed of by the Bewsey Male Voice Choir (Mr W Wright, conductor), Misses F Watson and Ada Hamer. Miss L Turton ably accompanied the artistes and at the close a comprehen- sive vote of thanks was heartily accorded all those who had taken part in the meeting, on the proposition of Bro. B Walker seconded by Bro W Wakefield. The celebrations will close to-day (Saturday) with a conferenoe of Invenile superintendents and workers in Bold-street schools, Warrington, at. 2.30 p.m., when a paver will be read by the fligh Superintendent, Bro R Bell. The following members of the Board will take part :—Bros S A Scarlett, J H Lear Caton, and A Tomlinson. After tea in Winmarleigh Cafe a public meeting will be held in the Bold-street schools when Mrs Dodds will preside.

Borough Police Court.

SATURDAY.—Before Mr. R. T. Fairclouah (in the chair), Alderman Tinnion, Messrs. A. J. Jolley and T Pemberton. GIVEN A CHANCE.—Annie Hmaiphreys, of Philip- street was charged on remand with having stolen a pair of boots and a tablecloth from the pawnshop, 75, Orford-lane, the property of Benedict Tyrer.—She was further charged at the instance of the N.S.P C.C. with neglecting her children. She pleaded guilty to the theft of the boots but denied the charge of neglecting ber children.—lt was stated that the woman had taken the boots from Mr. Tyrer's shop in Orford- Imre and had attempted to pawn them at a branch shop in Winwick-street. The boots bore the firm's private mark an d this led to the detection of the theft. During the adjournment it had been found that she had stolen the table-cloth. A number of pawn tickets were discovered in the house. Mr Browne prosecuted in the charge of child neglect, and said that tke woman had been under the super- vision of the society's officers for many years. Her husband had always given her good wages but sue bad continually pawned goods belonging to him. She owed her position to drink. She had promised that morning to give up her bad habit and reform, as also did her husband, who was not altogether blameless. Mr Browne suggested an adjournment of the case. For the theft the prisoner was sentenced to one day's imprisonment and the other case of neglect was adjourned for eight weeks. TtiESDAY.—Before Councillor C Jackson Homes (in the chair), and Colonel Edenton. He had carried out his promises to reform, better than any defendant they had been concerned with before. Inspector Wainwright reported that the husband had been at work sod had kept sober. He had visited the house and found the mother and children looking happy and contented. Rsilway Company's premises. Some loco sheds had been visited by P.C. Clarke, of the company's police, and Detective Goode, of the L. and N.-W. railway police, and the spades which bore the mark " Dallam Forge, Warrington " were found. Sometimes it was sixpence, and at other times the price of a drink. ACID STOMACHS ARE DANGEROUS.

NINE-TENTHS OF ALL STOMACH TROUBLE SAID TO BE DUE TO ACIDITY. A PHYSICIAN'S ADVICE ON CAUSE AND CURE.

A famous physician whose successful researches into the cause and cure of stomach and intestinal diseases have earned for him an international reputa- tion, said in the course of a recent lecture that nearly all intestinal troubles, as well as many diseases of the vital organs, were directly traceable to the deranged condition of the stomach, which in turn was due nine times out of ten to excessive acidity, which not only irritated and inflamed the delicate lining of the stomach, but also set up gastritis and stomach ulcers. It is interesting to note that he condemns the use of -patent medicines as well as of medical treatment for the stomach, stating that he and his colleagues have secured remarkable results by the use of ordinary bisurated magnesia, which, by neutralising the acidity of the food, removes the source of the trouble. He contends that it is as foolish to treat the stomach itself as it would be for a man who stepped on a tack to rub liniment on the foot without first removine the tack. Remove the tack and the foot will heal itself— neutralise the acid and stomach troubles will dis- appear. Irritating medicines and medical treatments are useless, so long as the contents of the stomach remain acid ; remove the acidity and there will be no need for medicine—the inflamed lining of the stomach will then heal itself. Sufferers from acidity should get a small bottle of bisurated magnesia from their chemist, and take half a teaspoonful in a quarter of a glass of hot or cold water after meals,. repeating in fifteen minutes, if necessary, this being the dose which the doctor has found moat efficacious in all cases.

===== 0002601_19121005_art0069.txt | included=true | included =====
The Examiner, Saturday, October 5, 1912.

===== 0002601_19121005_art0070.txt | included=true | included =====
THE MUNICIPALISATION OF HOSPITALS. _ Special to the" Examiner."

The most urgent social need of the pfesent time is the reorganisation of the medical pro- fession, and the adjustment of its relations to the general public. The National Insur- ance Act has done much to bring matters to I a crisis, but it would be a gross mistake to suppose that that much misrepresented measure has been the sole cause of the pre- sent discontent and unrest in the ranks of the medical profession. There have been two great underlying causes and the chief of these has been the immense scientific advance in the knowledge of the nature of disease and the processes of life during the past twenty years. So great has been this advance that few even in the ranks of the profession have been fully able to appreciate it. This has led to the apotheosis of pre- ventive medicine with its various powers of dealing with infectious disease, with the training and registration of midwives ane other hygienic measures all of which tend to diminish the work and the income of the average general practitioner. The other great underlying cause has been the economic changes of the same period, especially that form of minor socialism known as the joint stock enter- prise. Instead of following the spirit of the times in economic matters, the medical pro- fession has been compelled to revert to the crudest individualism with its inevitable soul killing competition. it is due to this that we have with us that form of cheap club practice so justly condemned by the recent Poor-Law Commission.

Up to about thirty years ago a qualified practitioner could by engaging unqualified assistants carry on a large practice, derive a good income, and have a fair amount of leisure to attend to other duties. The assist- ants carried on the routine of practice while the principal attended to special cases, to hospital and other public duties. This system largely aided the progressive develop- ment of the voluntary hospital system and many of the choicest fruits of medical science were nurtured and garnered by prominent men associated with the voluntary hospitals. The working of the Medical Act of 1858 and its subsequent amendments and additions gradually caused an abolition of the unqualified assistant until now they are as a body quite extinct and only fully qualified men exist. This has led to a great increase of individual practices and a corresponding diminution of assistants, and this in time has led to fierce and fiercer competition among qualified men,with disastrous results both to the public and the profession. While the profession has retrogressed in an economic sense the voluntary hospital system established under the old regime has developed in accordance with the spirit of the age along joint stock lines, and the same men who direct large commercial enterprises also direct—largely to their credit be it said —the voluntary hospital system. The medical profession as a body have no voice in the matter.

The National Insurance Act has brought the problem of the voluntary hospitals well to the front, and the other day at the con- ference of the British Hospitals Association, the Lord Mayor of Birmingham told his audience that sooner or later either the state or the municipalities would take over the hospitals. He preferred the•latter course. The task of taking over the voluntary hospitals is not so great as might be imagined. Already the State and the municipalities own and manage Poor Law and Fever Hospitals whose beds far out- number those of the voluntary hospitals, and if we view the lunatic asylums as mental hospitals the number of beds controlled now by the State and the municipalities is double those controlled by the voluntary hospital authorities. It is not generally recognised that there is no need for special legislation to deal with this important question. The Public Health Act passed by a Tory Government in 1875 provides for this under Section 131. There it is given that any local authority may build or acquire hospitals for the sick, and sickness has been held to include all forms of sickness whether infectious or not—even accidents. The hospitals at Widnes and Barry may be cited as examples. --

It is generally stated that the operation of the National Insurance Act will tend to cripple the voluntary hospitals by the with- drawal of subscriptions. Dr. Basil Rhodes, the secretary of the North Staffordshire Infirmary, is not so pessimistic ; he even believes that by suitable measures the sub- scriptions may be increased. Whether he be right or not the hospitals must be continued, extended and organised in accordance with the progress of scientific medicine ; that is largely in the direction of specialisation. The working of the Insur- ance Act in Germany proved the inadequacy of the voluntary hospitals there and the Insurance Authorities were compelled to build their own hospitals. The staffs of these hospitals are paid and the system runs smoothly without frantic appeals to the public for funds. In the near future the British public must make up its mind as to how its hospitals are to be sustained, extended and organised. Probably the course taken will be that of municipalisation with some degree of central control by the Local Government Board.

PALACE AND HIPPODROME.

Were we arranging the " bill " for the Hippodrome this week we should be inclined to give top place to Bert Lloyd in his sketch " What a Pal ! " It is the Ibest burglary act we have seen and is splendidly acted. Bert Lloyd's nervous intensity keeps one's pulses on the leap, and yet there are frequent breaks into sardonic humour which compel laughter. He is excellently supported by two other artistes, while his crib-cracking by the aid of electricity and the blow- pipe is most realistic. Cruikshank is a popular dish with Warrington audiences, and his drolleries never fail to louse the ready laugh. The original " story " worked no from the names of Warrington licensed houses is clever, but we don't admire the bathos of the sentimental little ditty about 'SMother." It is hardly worthy of Cruikshantee t ree 1) silver arch and break-away n •Anexceedingly ingly neat bar is i eatgymntrreentifturn on re siy avail and Bodine, lady and gentleman. They have struck out on a new line and the quality of their work will never fail to be appreciated. Comedy is predominant in the programme this week, in fact there is rather too much of a good thing with four comedians. Fred Ward is an old favourite, now assisted by his bright and youthful daughter Selina. Fred is still funny, but the youngster does a lot of the work. The simultaneous dancing of the pair is good, and the big boot dance a la Little Tich is remarkable for so young a performer as Selina. Wal Langtry has a style I entirely his own. His clerical make-up when allied I to some smart patter was irrestibly amusing, and the song with a refrain quite took the popular fancy. The I Enzer trio do some exceptionally smart juggling and I finish up with a convincing display. of jui-jitsu in which the lady shows remarkable self-defence against the attacks of a ruffian. Torn Collins has a good song and Beattie Browning makes a pleasing impression as comedienne and dancer.

MOTHER POISONS HER CHILDREN. At Moy Valley, Co. Kildare, the wife of a game- keeper poisoned herself and two children with rat poison, afterwards cutting their throats, and also her own. • When a doctor arrived one of the children was dead. Neither the mother nor the se-lond child is expected to recover.

THE NEW WESLEYAN CHURCH HOUSE AT WESTMINSTER.

On Thursday the new Wesleyan Methogist Church House at Westminster was oPeded with a dedicatory service. The building now completed was made possible by the Twentieth Century Fund inadgurated in 1898. One million guineas was the sum aimed at, and the amount received was £1,075,727, £242,206 of which was allocated

to the building fund. The principal features of the building are a Large Hall with seating accommodation for 2,500, a Small Hall seated for 600, the Library, and a grand Entrance Hall and Staircase. Numerous offices and committee rooms for the Church's work are situated in the pile.

in 1887, after 15 years' services with the company." He has been one or the pillars on which we have built up this great concern, and throughout the whole of his career he has devoted his time end attention most un- selfishly to your interest. As you know. ironworks have to live on terms of friendship with their neigh- bours, and Mr Bleckly's services as a diplomat and negotiator were in constant requisition. Ile was trea- surer of the Iron and Steel Institute for many years, and was one of the most prominent figures at their annual gatherings. Now, as regards this year, no aoubt you have studied the figures well, and will have noticed with satisfaction that we still exhibit the same caution in dividing our resources and in maintaining the strength of our position. We have had many difficulties to contend with during tbe past year, particularly by the unsettlement of labour and the coal strike. It is quite true that as regards

THE COAL STRIKE we have benefited by realizing all the coal we had on stock, and the little prophecy I made last year, recom- mending every consumer and householder to fill up his stocks or cellars to the fullest capacity, was well timed. (Hear, bear.) I hope all of you, at any rate, took full advantage of it, and did not suffer from shortness of coal during the strike. I did my best &t 0 bridge over the differences which arose with the miners, but the strike apparently had to come, and the new principle of the minimum wage had to be adopted. The minimum wage imposes considerable labour on our staff ; in fact, we have had to increase it, but there were not the discrepancies to the same extent between the wages and demands of the men in our Lancashire district, and, where rates have been levelled up to the amounts we were already paying,we gained some advantage in sales by equalizing cost with our competitors. Now, es regards the figures, you will observe on the capital and liabilities side that we have again strengthensd our reserve fund. The dividend which we distribute will pay you five per cent, for the year, and absorb £50,755 of the profits earned. Now, the directors have thought it wise to call the share- holders' special attention to the heavy charges which the ordinary shareholders have to pay in taxes, rates, and workmen's compensation, and they do this in order that they may emphasise the further burden the ordinary shareholders have to bear under the working of THE NATIONAL IN:iURANCK ACT. As stated, the effect of the Act represents a new tax of over one per cent per annum upon the amount of the ordinary capital, which is £610,000 ; in addition to which there will be heavy charges for extra clerks' time in order to keep up the immense amount of book work required. Now, I am not going to discuss the 'merits or demerits of the Act, or whether it will be advantageous generally to the nation or not. The point which must appeal to all employers is the unfair and inequitable burden placed upon the industrial com- panies who employ large numbers of men, and have a heavy wages net. The burden is most inequitable. The leverage of profits is based to such a large extent upon the total cost of an article sold, and not upon the 'wages only of the employed, that when the value of the raw material is lane, and wages small, the Insur- ance Act will fall much lighter than in those cases where the value of the raw material is low, and wages are high, and there the burden is very extreme. these Now of course, no one will object to carrying u schema if it is shown in any way how the amount which these schemes cost can be recovered, but so far I, at any rate, have not yet learned how this is to be done. In the coal trade it could be done, because we have not to meet competition elsewhere. Next year we shall he paying in taxes, rates, workmen's compensation and National Insurance

OVER £40,00) A YEAR, and whilst, as some politicians have said, we have only to add it to the cost of the article and the amoun.t charged disappears, yet my experience is that con- sumers are never too ready to pay increased costs; and unless times are bright, and demand is good, and competition disappears, the chance of getting back from your consumer the added cost of any article sold is very slight. At any rate at the present time neither Great Britain nor its Governmelt assist any industrial ooncern or individual to recoup the heavy charges which they so constantly impose. Moneys are lightly and airily spent and increase the popularity. of the politician at very serious cost to the working population all° have to pay for them. I saw in the paper the other day that the taxes per capita of Great Britain compare very unfavourably with the taxes. of other nations. In the United Kingdom the charge IS £3 4s lid a head against £3 2a Cd in France, £2 5s CA in the Netherlands' £1 14s 4d in Germany, and LI us 2rl in Belgium. Nov; most of these nations are ever on the watch to assist the industries of their country, but I do not thiuk there is anyone who can give any particular instance of any assistance, in any shape or form, given by the Govern- ment of this country to those who do so much

MR. W. PETER RYLANDS AND THE INSURANCE ACT'. Mr. W. PETER RYLANDS seconded. In doing so he commented upon the burdens imposed upon the great producing companies, such as the Pearson and Knowles Coal and Iron Company, by taxes, rates, the Workmen's Compensation Act, and now, in addition, the National Insurance Act. It was a burden which, in the case of industries that were directly in competition with foreign manufacturers, could not be recovered from the cost. In the latter case the burdens had either got to be borne by the workmen in a reduction of wages or to come from the pockets of the shareholders. No new company could start with any hope of success under existing conditions, and when investors could get four, five, or six per cent, on their money when invested abroad, without any anxiety whatever, and with absolute security, there was no inducement to put their money into such a perilous business as a great producing undertaking like that which receives all the kicks and blows of the community and very little encouragement indeed. The result was that as time went on their competitors who were not fit must fall by the way. The burdens to which reference had been made could rot be put on the r houlders of the unfit, or if they were, the unfit could not survive. The Pearson and Knowles Coal and Iron Company would go on more and more efficiently, relying upon the support of the shareholders in pursuing the policy of setting on one side capital, in the form of a reserve fund to meet the burdens which the Government imposed upon them. If that policy was successful, and the hopes of the future were assured to the country, he believed with confidence they might look forward to the Pearson and Knowles Company standing out as a great landmark in the history of England for many years to come. ELECTION OF DIRECTORS, The CHAIRMAN moved that Mr W Peter Rylands be re-elected a director of the company, and said that one of the great sources of their profit was Rylands Bros, Ltd, which v.as principally in the hands of Mr Rylands. It WWI one of their great assets that they had him as a director. (Hear, hear, and applause.) Mr BRANCKER seconded, and the motion was carried. Mr RYLANDS thanking the meeting for their renewed confidence in him.

The CHAIRMAN proposed the election of Mr John "met,tv, jun. as a director of the company to act with his father. He was worthy of the position. Mr W PETER RYLANDS seconded, and the motion was carried. Mr JOHN PLECKLY, jilt), said it would be his constant endeavour to serve the company to the best of his ability, and he thanked the Chairman for the kind manner in which he had spoken of him. Mr JOHN J BLECKLY proposed that Captain Harmood- Banner be elected a director of the company, Mr H S BLECKLY seconded the motion, which was also carried unanimously. THE AUDITORS. The CHAIRMAN, in acknowledgment, said all the directors were doing their best for the company. The new works at Partington, whilst they had been the cause of immense expenditure, were going to be one of the finest things in LinchshirP. They were going to do grand things for Warrington in adding to the trade at the existing works, and they were very proud of every- thing connected with them. They were alto very proud of the fact that the main erections at Partington had been carried out by this Company. (Applause).

Bold-street Wesleyan Church. The harvest thlanksgiving services were held at this church on Sunday, and were largely attended. The church had been prettily decorated by the lady members of the congregation- In the morning the Rev Hugh Williamson preached from the text, " Although the 11g tree shall not blossom, neither shall fruit he in the vines ; the labour of the olive shall fail, and the fields shall yield no meat ; the flock shall be cut off from: the fold, and there shall be no herd in the stalls, yet I will rejoice in the Lord. I will joy in the God of my salvation." (flabakuk iii., 17 ho d 18). In the evening the Rev W A Beckett occupied the pulpit, and based his remarks on the words " Be not ye therefore like unto them, for your Father knoweth what things ye have need of before ye ask him." The choir efficiently rendered the anthems, " Father of Mercies " in the morning, and in the evening " Sun of my soul," Miss E Taylor being the soloist. Mr Frank Hickman pie- sided at the organ.

Workingmen's Mission Bank Quay. Harvest Thanksgiving Services were held at the above Mission on Sunday the preacher being Mr II C Marsh, B.A. of Hartley College, Manchester. The services were well attended, many persons having to be refused admission at night, owing to the Chapel being so crowded. In the afternoon a musical service was held. Solos were rendered by Miss jEva Tanner, soprano,and Mr Edward Pownall, bass, the latter being heard to esnecial advantage in the selection " Honour and Arms" (from "Samson.") The choir gave various items, perhaps the most effective being the rendering of Mendelsohn's " Hear my prayer," the solo being feel- ingly sung by Miss Tanner, Mr Geo Dale presided over the afternoon service, and a short address was given by Mr. H. G. Marsh. The collections were for the chapel funds, and amounted to £7 4s. 6d. Mr. F. Atkinson conducted the choir, and Mr. T. Tanner presided at the organ. The chapel had been tastetully decorated for the occasion.—On Monday evening a social was held in the schoolroom, the proceeds of which (£2 tis.) were for the Mission League of Help Fund. Miss Winstanley (soprano) and Miss C Jones (contralto) rendered songs in a pleasing manner, and Miss Eva Whitby gave a pianoforte solo very credit- ably for one so young. Miss Alice Taylor was warmly applanded for her recitations. Mr. N. Hough presided over the concert, and Mr. T. Dale moved a vote of thanks to all who had contributed to the gathering.

St. John's Presbyterian Church. " Home Life and Its Relationships."

Preaching at St. John's on Sunday evening- last on the subject of " Home life and its relationships," Mr. Reid said there entered into home life an element of neceessity in which every life was caught, the con- dition, place, connections, all being things existent before its advent, yet serving to influence it to a great extent. There was also the element of liberty evidenced in the creation of new family ties, which in turn became the conditions of necessity into which new lives made their advent. These facts pointed out how the sovereignty of God and free will of man were ' inseparable and continued to work in a conjunction with each other that was indissoluble. The world, continued the preacher, owed more than it was await) of to many of its men and women who had never married. The potential parenthood never realised did , not isolate them from the race, nor render them in any sense imperfect any more than it did to Jesus, against whom it had been urged as a defect in His example. He and they had lifted parenthood into a higher plane. When Adam's natural children were sick and lost, Jesus whose fatherly heart responded in sympathy, took them for his own, and to all men and women who, owing to. various causes, took up the same position in connection with marriage as did Jesus, and to all husbands and wives whose homes were childless, came God's message to see in that, not merely an incident, but His call to see Jesus, in that also, as a perfect examole. It was possible to have spiritual children even as Paul claimed Timothy. Speaking of love between parents and children Mr. Reid claimed Jesus as the one wh) bad lifted up the child, and made the affection a deeper, tenderer and more refined thing than existed where He , was known, and the subtle fragrance which pervaded the true home was the result of that presence there. The prea3her spoke of the home as the first church of the child, opening its doors to excommunicated and persecuted sons of the church down the centuries. 1 Proceeding to deal further with the relationship between parent and child, Mr Reid denounced the words and actions of many parents which savoured of proprietary right in their offspring, stating parents had no such right in their children. A right to expect their obedience they had, but the child also had rights, the right that every parent should do his duty by him. The greatest responsibility a parent had to his child was to train him not to look after number one, but to discover his own powers, to control them, to realise that not one was superfluous, but necessary in the service of God and man. There came .a time when parental authority must cease, the object of parental training being to render the child independent and nothing roused the indignation of Jesus more; than

A LITTLE STORY WHICH IS AN ALLEGORY.

Once on a time a little leaf was heard to sigh and cry,as leaves often do when a gentle wind is about. And the twig said "What is the matter, little leaf?" "The wind," said the leaf, "just told me that one day it would pull me off, and throw me to the ground to die." The twig told it to the branch, and the branch told it to the tree. And when the tree heard it it rustled all over, and sent word back to the leaf : "Do not be afraid, hold on tightly, an you shall not go off till you want to." And so the leaf stopped sighing, and went on singing and rustling. And so it grew all summer till October. And when the bright days of autumn came, the leaf saw all the leaves around becoming very beautiful. Some were yellow and some were scarlet, and some were striped with colours. Then it asked the tree what it meant. And the tree said : _

"All these leaves are getting ready to fly away, and they have put on these colours because of their joy." Then the little leaf began to want to go, and grew very beautiful in thinking of it. And when it was very gay in colours, it saw that the branches of the tree had no colours in them, and so the leaf said : "Oh, branch, why are you lead-coloured and we golden ? " "We must keep on our work-clothes," said the tree, "for our work is not yet done; but your clothes are for a holiday, because your task is over."

_ Just then a little puff of wind came, and the leaf let go without thinking of it, and the wind took it up and turned it over, and then let it fall gently down under the edge of a fence among hundreds of leaves, and it never waked up to tell what it dreamed about.

THIS WEEK'S STORY. A CRICKET JOKE.

At a league cricket match a young fellow had the misfortune to get several of his teeth broken whilst playing against the opponents' fast bowler. In the return fixture, when the young man was again facing the fast bowler, and who was about to deliver the ball, he shouted across to the bowler: "1 say,matey, I hope you are not after my teeth again ?" "No, my lad," came the quick reply, "it's the stumps I'm after this time."

RASH ALL OVER BOY'S HEAD.

Grouts Cottage, Tollesbunt D'Arcy, Essex, Hog.— When my little boy was about three weeks old he had a rash break out all over his head. Then it spread on his face, then on to the front of his legs. All the top of his- head was craved with a thick coating of what looked like scurf. Then his face and legs broke out with little tiny red pimples, sometimes very red and inflamed and always itching. At times we had hard work keeping him from scratching him- self all to pieces. He was in continual torment from irritation. He was treated for two months, and we tried various other remedies, but nothing did him any good. He gradually got worse, until we had no rest with him night or day. "At last I tried Cuticura Soap and Cuticura Oint- ment and the first time I used them he found relief. After using three cakes of Cuticura Soap and four boxes of Cuticura Ointment he was completely cured, and has been for three months. He has cut six teeth without any signs of it returning, and people who saw him said he would be sure to have it through teething." (Signed) Mrs. H. Scott, Jan. 23, 1912. WARRINGTON NONCONFORMITY.

ITS 250th TtNNIVERSARY

===== 0002601_19121005_art0087.txt | included=true | included =====
Concerning Nonconformist Chapels in General and Cairo St. Chapel in particular.

SANKEY STREET T

The modern designation "Cairo-street Chapel " obscures the fact that the meeting house was originally approached from Sankey-street and stood in quietness some distance from it. As described in early legal documents it was "near to Sankey- street and approached by a passage three yards broad." It was not until 1846 when Cairo-street was made that it began to enjoy its present prominence. Much interest attaches both to the site and to the chapel some of which it will be our great pleasure to discover to the reader.

===== 0002601_19121005_art0088.txt | included=true | included =====
SANKEY-STREET TWO CENTURIES AGO.

At the close of the seventeenth century a certain Randle Myddletom carried on the business of blacksmith in the centre of Warrington. His smithy straggled out into Sankey-street upon the site now partly filled by the Union Bank, but nine feet beyond the present building line. Behind the smithy stood his dwelling, and in an open space a brewhouse and convenient pump; beyond this his barn. A passage three yards wide at the side of the smithy led to a piece of open land extending south- wards fifty yards in length and sixteen yards in breadth. There is theisuggestion that the blacksmith did a little farming in the fact that the land was bounded on one side by "Handle Myddleton's barne."

Like Nonconformists the country over, the Warrington congregation showed not the

===== 0002601_19121005_art0089.txt | included=true | included =====
*0 CENTURIES AGO.

a fine stair, and at the back a lofty panelling surmounted by a sounding board. The preacher stood behind a large book desk, his Bible resting upon a great red cushion. The pulpit was not placed at the end, but against one of the longer walls of the build- ing. The gallery ran the length of the wall opposite and where needful filled also the two other sides. A clock adorned the centre of the gallery, usually black faced with gilded fingers and figures, and with a sepulchral tick that struck solemnity into young hearts. THE PREACHER'S HAT. Into such an abode of studied reverence it

seems almost impossible to realise that a comic element might enter. But comicality there surely must have seemed in the ceremony of the preacher's hat. Upon the panelling which formed the pulpit back a peg was placed or a brass nail driven in. On the occasion of a funeral sermon the minister's hat with its silken mourning band was carried and hung up in the presence of the congregation. Dr. Halley tells an amusing story of a mourning incident witnessed in his youth. Some alterations had been in progress and the peg had been removed. It was a funeral service, and the bereaved family was of importance. The congregation had gathered and the undertaker, according to custom, carried the minister's plumed hat to the pulpit. It was only then discovered

'OXIRb-STREET CHAPEL.

least unwillingness to worship in hidden places, and the quiet croft or orchard lying away from Sankey-street seemed quite fitted to their needs.

The deed by which the land was secured is dated Juno 17th, 1703, "in the second yeare of the raigne of our Sovereign Lady Anne," and sets forth that the Earl of Warrington rented to the minister, Dr. Charles Owen, "all that late erected building or meeting house situate on a certain parcell of ground on the backside of a messuage or dwelling-house in Sankey-street . . . at an annual rent of two shillings payable at Midsummer and Martinmass by equal portions."l The description of the chapel as "newly built" shows that it had taken form, but no clue is given as to its size or cost. After the fashion of the period the lease is based upon a period of three lives and the common plan of choosing young lives was followed. The names are "Samuel, son of John Bent, Apothecary, John, son of Thomas Mather, Woollen Droper, and Thomas, son of Martha Percival], widow."

EARLY STREET IMPROVEMENT. Sixteen years later Dr. Owen purchased all the land occupied by Randle Myddleton. The blacksmith's house became the home of the minister and was occupied by Dr. Charles Owen. The smithy which made itself so. conspicuous by its obtrusion upon Sankey' street was sold " to the Town of Warrington to be pulled down." This early instance of street improvement is decidedly interesting. Indeed the entire picture called up by the old deeds is most attractive to the student of Old Warrington. The smithy, at which both Cavalier and Roundhead had watched their horses shod during the civil war, with pump farm and brewhouse lurking modestly in the background make a picture too good to be forgotten. It was Old Warrington indeed. Would that it had been transferred to canvas before the street improvements I began. And in a town which has known just a little concerning street improvement a comparison of prices then and now would have decided interest. EARLY NONCONFORMIsT CHAPELS.

A general gossip upon the earliestiNoncon- formist meeting-houses will best introduce the chapel built in Sankey street. . .

In general design the chapels were plain and even sombre, built with a studied avoid- ance of what was scornfully known as 'papistry.' Many were built cheaply of timber, but most had plain brick walls, a I flagged roof, substantial galleries and pews. Where means were forthcoming the pews, galleries and pulpit were of oak or walnut, with the luxury of cushions and curtains. Ornament had no value; possibly, from their view it was inconsistent with spiritual worship. Of symbolism there was none save that sometimes the figure of a dove was carved above the pulpit or upon its front. Needless to say that everything that flavoured of sensuous worship wasicarefully avoided, but in their studied protest against the old superstitions they often erected buildings that wore a pall of gloom. The windows •were seldom large, and plain massive woodwork created a sense of heaviness. A fine example of the early meeting-house may be studied at Chowbent, but this has the luxury of a bell turret from which the worshippers were summoned to prayer. Chester has the fine chapel in which Matthew Henry preached, with a pulpit of out- standing and massive beauty.

THE PULPIT. This last feature was common to nearly all the old chapels. As though to indicate the high importance attached to the preaching of the gospel the Nonconformists lavished their best upon the pulpits. Usually there was

that there was no peg to receive it. There was a retreat to the vestry and a hasty con- sultation. Then the undertaker in the presence of a waiting throng appeared with a hammer and nail, and with blows that rudely violated the calm of the sanctuary provided a place for the precious emblem of mourning. The vestry had evidently decided it better to break the Commandment against doing any manner of work on the Sabbath day than to violate the proprieties of chapel mourning. In front of the pulpit a reading desk some- times stood from which the preacher led the devotions. The feelingiwhich lay behind this arrangement seems to have been that the minister should pray as one of his flock and in their midst before climbing the steps to act as teacher.

===== 0002601_19121005_art0092.txt | included=true | included =====
THE TABLE PEW.

There was usually a large table, used at the Lord's Supper, and commonly holding a large Bible and copy of Foxe's 'Book of Martyrs. When children were to be christened the table was adorned by a basin, for the font was classed among the "idolatrous gear" of the papists and was disallowed. Round this was what was called the Table Pew,which formed another peculiar feature. The large space was occupied by the poor male members of the congregation. It was usually full, for if there were a vacancy a deacon would bring some one from the back benches to fill the gap. The curious thing is that it was reserved for men.

The seats appear to have been appropriated to individuals for a curious ground plan of the 'old Birch Chapel at Itusholme shows twelve spaces marked with the name of twelve poor men.

===== 0002601_19121005_art0093.txt | included=true | included =====
THE FAMILY PEWS.

The family pew system was established from the first. Some pews were square apartments to which curtains gave almost complete seclusion. Sometimes the family pew had a servants' pew adjoining, a green curtain dividing them. From pew rents a large part of the Church's income was received and the families who paid the rent came to regard the pews as their freehold to trespass upon which would have been regarded as a serious intrusion.

the allocation of sittings was a matter of some importance and their relative values were carefully fixed. A concern that a sufficient rent should be paid is revealed in many Church records some of which read rather quaintly in this later day. There is, for instance, a memorandum made by the Warrington congregation; May 14th 1797. Many persons occupying seats in the Dissenting Chapel who pay n 3 Rent or not a Rent adequate to the value of the seats, in consequence of which persons cannot be accommodated with such seats as they wish who are willing to pay a fair Rent for them We the Trustees of the said Chapel do hereby determine that no double seats shall be c,nsidered as rented for which One Guinea a year at least is not paid, neither shall any single seats be considered as rented for which half a guinea a year at least is not paid. The persons who at present occupy any seats at a less Rent than is mentioned above may continue to sit in them till there is an opportunity of letting them at the above prices when they must remove to another seat, except they are willing to pay the above rents themselves. The one guinea per annum is mentioned as the Rent. In Double Seats it is expected that the best Doable seats should pay more than that price. (Signed) Ellis Bent,Roger Gaskell, Isaac Turner, Samuel Gaskell, Thomas Turner. But it 'should not be forgotten that each Chapel had its "poor seats" for the use of which no contribution was expected,and that the "Table Pew" was also the privilege of poor men.

PEW FARMING EXTRAORDINARY. An extraordinary incident arising from the pew-renting habit accurs in the annals of Cross-street Chapel, Manchester. When

that fine meeting-house was built in 1693 two members of the congregation offered, as a speculation of their own, to inserta gallery. They were to build and maintain the gallery appropriating for themselves all that was received from the pew rents. This offer was accepted and a formal agreement was drawn under which the speculative pair covenanted to give a full I account to the trustees each year of their income and expenditure. Whether the arrangement was of long or brief duration we have not discovered, but balance sheets as described in the agreement were prepared and received.

===== 0002601_19121005_art0094.txt | included=true | included =====
THE SINGING GALLERY.

The most unexpected development in Non- conformity was the "Singing Gallery." This did not come early, but the course of a generation saw it widely established. 3reak- ing curiously from Puritan usage they introduced the violin, flute and bass-viol. Along with the "singing men and women" the musicians occupied usually the central part of the front gallery, and whilst adorn- ing this conspicuous place gave a lead to the singing. _

Though unexpected among the children of the Puritans, the development was a blessing undenied. The minister's habit in the early days was to read out one or two lines from a Psalm and leave the congregation to saw their way through the words after him. Young Isaac Watts, blessed from above with ,an ear for musie, began to give the Noncon- formists something that could really be sung, and the "singing men and women" that sprung up everywhere were his disciples. Outwardly the meeting houses were plain and usually showed little attempts at adorn- ment. Sometimes a bell-turret gave dis- tinction. Oftener a dial placed upon the wall was the only adornment. The dial might bear a Bible text. Some- times as at Blackley a solemn verse arrested the passer-by. My charge is sure, it may be soon, Each hastening minute leads me on ; The awful summons draweth nigh, And every day I live, I die. Around Manchester a belt of such meeting- , houses quickly sprang up. Those at Black- ' ley, Gorton, Dob Lane, Birch, Monton, Dean Row, were typical country chapels. Cross- street, Manchester, represents Nonconformity at its wealthiest and best.

Knutsford and Chowbent have chapels to which it is well worth while to go on pilgrimage, and no visitor to Chester should miss the fine old meeting house made famous by Matthew Henry, May these fine old memorials be long preserved if only to keep in memory the courage and sacrifice of those who built them. To be Continued. A.M.

RUSSIAN CRUISER AT SPITHEAD

On Wednesday the Russian cruiser Ole arrived at Spithead from the Baltic for a two days stay. She saluted the National and Admiral's flag, and the flag- ship Victory replied.

Are Tar-sprayed Roads Dangerous? The monthly meeting of the Warrington Rural Dis- trict Council was held in Bewsey Chambers,on Wednes- day afternoon, Mr G Mather presiding. There were also present : Rev A M Mitchell, Messrs W Furness, A Brooks, P Longton, Evelyn Sutton (deputy clerk), B S Newcombe (surveyor), H H Burnage (assistant surveyor), F Kerfoot (inspector), and Dr Sephton (medical officer).

PIDGATE SEWERACIR SCITENIR.

A small committee consisting of -theWaTrman, Mr IV Furness, Mr Brooks and Dr Murray, was formed to go into the matter of the Padgate Sewerage scheme.

PROPOSED NEW BRIDOR AT PIDO ATE. The Council considered a suggestion of the Cheshire Lines Railway Company to erect a substantial road bridge at Padgate in place of the present wooden bridge and two level crossings. The Council passed a resolution in favour of the principle of the suggestion. MEDICAL OFFICER'S REPORT. The Medical Officers report which was adopted showed that during the month there had been 16 births and 17 deaths. The death rate was equal to one of 14 per 1,000 as against 36 per 1,000 for the last three months and 207 for the corresponding month last year. There were no deaths in the Rixton district.

He said that horses were slipping on them continually and he would like to know if anything could be done during the winter months. IRON TRADE ACTIVITY. HIGHER WAGES FOR PUDDLERS. The prosperous state of the iron and steel trades le reflected in the return of the accountants to the Mid- land Iron and Steel Wages Board, which was published on Saturday. The Board regulates wages in Stafford- shire, Lancashire Yorkshire, Worcestershire, and South Wales, and the return covers the output of iron by the seventeen selected firms during July and August. According to this the average net selling price of all descriptions of iron was lOs 3d per ton, compared with £7 4s 9d In the previous two months, or an advance of 5s fid per ton. Compared with July and August last year, the present figures show an increase in the selling price of not less than Li Is 5d per ton. In accordance with the sliding scale, the wages for puddling are increased 3d per ton,mahing the rate 98 9d. In addition the men are to receive a bonus of 64 per ton in accordance with arrangement with the employer. arrived at in July last. This brings the rata up to 103 3d per ton, as against 6s 9cl during the whole of last year. The total output of iron during July and August was 41,226 tons, an increase of 2,268 tons com- pared with the previous two months.

ONTARIO PEACHES IN LONDON.

The Deputy Minister of Agriculture for the Province of Ontario has advised the resident agent in London, Mr. N. B. Co!cock, by cable, that the Provincial Government is again supervising the shipment of Ontario peaches for the British market. It was expected that one grower alone will forward 7,000 cases and this number represents double the quantity for- warded last year. In recent years, the peach industry of Ontario has immensely increased, and to-day 95 per cent, of all the peaches grown in Canada are to be found on Ontario fruit farms. Good peach land planted with trees costs from £5O per acre; unplanted, itcan be bought from £l2 per acre. Trees are brought to bear- ing in the fourth year and should last 15 years. Official figures state that the net profit per sore is as high as £55. Enquiries at Convent Garden, London, show that the fruit is in much demand and meeting with a ready sale. Peaches are packed in boxes of eighteens and twenties and arrive in good condition.

THE DISTRICT NURSES.

HOW THE INSURANCE ACT WILL BENEFIT THE LOCAL ASSOCIATION.

A Hint to Friendly Societies.

"With regard to the Nursing Association, I venture to think that the Insurance Act, instead of being a hindrance, will be of enormous assistance," said Mr. W. Peter Rylands in the course of his presidential address at the annual meeting of the Workingmen's Committee of the Warrington District Nursing Associa- tion held at the Town Hall on Saturday afternoon, lie said it was quite impossible at this time, whenever men ' were met together in connection with charitable objects or business endeavour, to avoid some reference to a change in the conditions which must have a pro- found effect upon both those activities of life. Ile referred,of course,to the National Insurance Act. So far as the principles that underlay the Act were concerned, he had never disguised his opinion that the way in which the funds were raised was not sound. That, how- ever, did not affect them at that meeting. There could be no doubt that men gathered in connection with an institution of that kind must view with the keenest I sympathy the objects of the Act, and with the strongest possible belief that the benefits to be derived from it— however faulty its methods in economic respects— must be of incalculable benefit to the people among whom they would be distributed. When they had to consider the effect of the Act upon Infirmary contributions and the like, they had on occasion said they were a little bit anxious and worried as to whether it might not press hardly upon institutions of that kind. That was a question upon which the future alone could enlighten them. He did not want for a moment to meet trouble half way. Of coarse, while the measure was being discussed as a Bill, they naturally put forward their anticipated objections. how that it was an Act, they roust make the best of it and be sincerely hoped the funds of the Infirmary would not suffer. But with regard to the Nursing Association, he ventured to think that the Act, instead of being a hindrance, would be of

===== 0002601_19121005_art0109.txt | included=true | included =====
ENORMOUS ASSISTANCE.

The Nursing Association, he took it, was concerned more with the alleviating of temporary sickness than with the serious illnesses of life which were dealt with at great cost in the hospitals. The Nursing Association from the point of view of temporary assistance, accome plished more at less cost than any institution of an analogous description. It stood to reason that every body—by which be meant an approved society—which had to pay out funds to those who were sick must regard it as a matter of vital importance to them that there should he a Nursing Association to help to bring back those sick people to the list of healthy ones and so put an end to the benefits which the approved societies would have to pay. It was most important that those who were con- nected with approved societies—most of them run by workingmen—should realise from the very outset that prevention was better than cure. It was far better to prevent a man being sick or to cure him qiickly than to go on paying him comparatively large SUM of money every week to keep him in eickness. At the present time the contribmioss which were handed to the trees :Ter of that committee from the friendly societies in the town, amounted to a paltry £lO a year. It no doubt was not paltry under the conditions that prevailed pre- viously, when friendly societies had great difficulties in making both ends meet and were anxious to make their bee( fits vs ratisfactory as they could. But now that the friendly secieties Lad got within their ranks practically every workii gman in the town' their membership had not merely doubled or trebled but he had no doubt it had muitiplied six or seven fold. "'hey bad compulsory ccntributions and large sums would be handed to themes subsidies from the employers and the State. They would now be able to conduct heir operations on lines which they vould probably have liked to do but hitherto could not afford, and lines which business prudence would dictate. He ventured to suggest that the representatives of friendly troc'etiee should keep that point in mind rni insist, from a purely business point of view, that their organ- sations,R hatever they did with Infirmaries, should take Advantage of the Act to assist themselves by contribut- leg as handsomely as possible to the funds of the Nursing Association. Having pointed out that the work ot the association was outePe the benefits of the Act and that it. was of untold benefit te the working- men, Mr Itylands said they believed that the Act, instead of diminishing the resources of the association, would tend to strengthen them. Tnere was a reir attendance of represen'atives Of the _t• -osep..., and various works. The Mayor (Councillor n hl d Alderman Smethurst were also present, together with the following officers of the association :—Mr George Walker (chairman), Mr II Hope (hon. treasurer), and Mr J Fargher (hon. secretary). The statement of accounts showed that the amount raised during the year ended June 30th, 1912, was £242 7a Gri es against £3ll 12a 11d. After deducting expenses, the sum of £236 153 was handed over to the treasurer of the Nursing Association Last year the contribution was £367 fis 7d. It was pointed out that the nurses had made neatly 15,000 visits and given their services to 595 patients during the past year, most of whom had been skilfully nursed through serious illness. The nurser were a valuable asset to the town aid neigh- bourhood and gave their help freely. Regular subscrip- tions of say one penny monthly from all the working- men and women in the district would realise about one-half the amount required for upkeep of the association. Collecting boxes would be supplied on application to wo-ks, Ste. where a more systematic collection could not be arranged. Commenting upon the balance sheet, Mr Ryland said that while the falling off in the total amount raised was to he regretted, it was gratifying to observe that every year since the inception of the committee the contributions from the works—upon which the com- mittee chiefly depended for its prosperity and perman- ence—had shown an increase. (Applause.) During the past year the works subscriptions totalled £163 las 9d las compared eith £157 3s lid in 1911. The reduction in the total contribution to the central fund sits entirely accounted for by a decrease in the proceeds of special efforts which most necessarily be spasmodic. THE LATE MR. F. MONKS. The late Mr Monks did much for the town during his business career and he alwa3s showed his sympathy not only with the Nursing Association bat with the 'EIS mary and any charity of a similar kind. Mr Rylands concluded by moving a vote of ccndolence with the family. BRASS BAND FESTIVAL.

ST. HILDA COLLIERY'S VICTORY.

No fewer than 200 bands competed on Saturday in the thirteenth annual contest among the leading brass bands for the championship prize of 1,000 guineas and eight other prizes at the Cr3stsl Palace. The majority of the bands came from Lancashire and Yorkshire, and considerable surprise was expressed that last year's championa—Crosfleld's Perfection Soap Works Band (Warringtoo)—did not put in an appearance, though entered on the list. The result of the contest was St. Hilda's Colliery, Durham ; 2, Irwell Springs ; 3, Foden's Wagon Works; 4, Black Dike; 5, Wingate's Temperance; 6, South Moor Colliery; 7, liebdou Bridge. The test piece prescribed for the whole of the per- formers was a beautiful selection from "William Tell," arranged by Mr. W. Rimmer, and the adjudicators, Lieutenant Charles Godfrey, 51. V 0., Lieutenant J Ord Hume, Lanoashire Fusilier. and Mr G II Mercer, must have had considerable dlfilluity in making the arrards, so equsl were the merits of several of the performances. The winning team, St Hilda Colliery, Durham, were the winners of the championship for the first time, though they had previously competed at the Palace on several occasions Their victory created a tremendous amount of enthusiasm among their supporters and their conductor, Mr W llslllwell, was warmly congratulated. heel' Springs had carried off the trophy in 1005 sue 1908. Forlen's Motor Works won it iu 190, and Black Dike Mills in 1902.

A NEW BOOK ON HYGIENE. By Dr. Hutt, Late Assistant M. 0.11. for Warrington.

Messrs. P. S. Ring and Son, of London, have just published "Hygiene for Health Visitors, School Nurses and Social Workers," by Dr C W Hutt, the late assistant Medical Officer of Health for Warrington, and now senior school doctor, Brighton Education Com- mittee. The work (which is dedicated to the Mayor of Warrington, Dr Joseph and the Chairman of the Health Committee, Alderman James Evans) is a practical text- book for all interested in the social welfare and better- ment of the poorer classes;more especially for those who take up special positions in public work. Public bodies are becoming more and more insistent on their officers having had special training bearing directly on the duties of their posts. The Examination of the Royal Sanitary Institute is admirably fitted for the purpose of ascertaining whether the candidates possess suitable knowledge for such posts, and this book, which largely illustrated, follows closely the syllsbus of this examination. The book comprises in one volume information which up to the present has bad to be sought in several text-books. A brief survey of the work convinces us that Dr. Hutt bas,to use a hackneyed phrase, supplied a longfelt want. He takes the student through a wide course of reading, none of it abstruse, and all helpful, from the elements of physiology and their epplicntion to ventilation, warming and lighting, drainage, care of infants and young children, elements of home nursing, first aid, duties of health visitors end school nurses, Fanitary legal knowledge etc. These are enly a few of the headings of chapters in a thoughtfully compiled and helpfully illustrated work. The price is 7s. 6d. net.

INSURANCE ACT. CARDS AT TIIE END OF THE QUARTER. The attention of the Insurance Commissioners has teen drawn to the fact that the requirement of the regulations that employers should return the health insurance contribution cards of their employees fully stamped up to date immediately upon the expiration of the period of currency of the cards may cause incon venience to employers whose custom it is to keep one week's wages or part thereof in hand. In order to meet this difficulty tto Commissioners have eocidei to alter the regulations by extending the time within which the cards mast be stamped and returned. The effect of the alteration made is that cards must be returned to the workers not less than six days after tbe expiration of their period of currency. The cards for the first quarter, which expires on October 13, must therefore be stamped and returned by the following Saturday, October 19. It should be pointed out that this in no way affects the req,drement that cards mast in all CIIS^S be stamped `.efore payment of wages for the period in respect of ,hich the contributions are payable.

UNHAPPY ROYAL MARRIAGE.

We reproduce above portraits of Prince George of Bavaria and his wife, the Arch- duchese Isabella, a daughter of the Arch- duke Frederic of Austria, who have separ- ated after only a few months of married life, the young bride having returned to

her father's palace at Vienna. The cause of the quarrel is said to be a question of rank and precedence, the Princess feeling dissatisfied with the place allotted to her . among Bavarian Roya tice. Prince George is 31 years of age, while his wife is 23.

MR. HAROLD SMITH'S EXPERIENCE. - Home Ruler at a Unionist Meeting.

WORKINGMAN'S UNEXPECTED SPEECH.

Mr. Harold Smith, M.P., experienced a surpiise at a Conservative meeting on Tuesday night at Penrbyn Hall, Bangor. In the course of his address against Homo Rule he was intertupted bi an Irish working man in the audience, who promptly accepted an invitation to come on the platform and state his case for Home Rule.

Mr Smith said that in these days of tottering thou- sands in Liberal majorities the Unionist in no con- stituency need despair, even in the Carnarvon Boroughs (applause), and with such a candidate as Mr Austin Jones they might hope to place the Chancellor in a position where be would have lt isnre to study these it tricacies of finance of which he was at present entirely ignorant.(Applause.) Proceeding Mr, Smith referred to his experience in Ulster last week,and sp le of Ulster's opposition to the 'infamous" deter- mination to place them under the tyrannical control of their opponent. He appealed, as Unionists, hi?' said, had all along appealed, to the principle of justice and fairplay of his audience in considering this infamous proposal. He refused to believe that the English people would sanction such a proposal if they got an opportunity of expressing their views. Radical papers called those who adv. cated Ulster's cause traitors. If these men were traitors, he would

RATHER RE A TRAITOR WITH CHARLIE BERESFORD THAN A LOYALIST WITH WINSTON CIICRCIIIIL.

Loud applause') At this stage a man in the audience shouted excitedly, and walked out. Mr. Smith remarked, 'lf you have a Radical present, he doesn't like the truth," Whereupon the man turned back and asked to be allowed to address the audience. Mr. Smith at once invited him on the platform, and the man, amid cheers, promptly mounted the stage and addressed the audience. He was a working man named Sheeran, well known in Bangor. He was evidently illiterate, bur he spoke with great earnestness. ULSTER'S NARROW-MINDEDNESS.

In effect he said Mr. smith's remarks applied not to Irishmen, but to gentlemen in Ireland. All this Ulster business, he said, was narrow-mindedness on the part of Unionists, as was shown by their refusal to listen to Mr. Winston Churchill. In the wildest places they would at least listen tc a man who came to speak to them, but they would not allow a man to come to Ulster. But there were thousands in Ireland more than in Ulster looking for their rights. Con- cluding, Sheeran said he was not used to public speaking, and, besides, he was interrupting the meeting. As he passed off the platform Mr. Smith hhook hands with him amid cheers, and, as the man walked down the hall, remarked : " Now you have got the whole Radical case for Horne Rule in behind " (Laughter.) Concluding, Mr. Smith appealed to all to support the cause of Ulster.

MEDICAL M.P. CONDEMNS ALCOHOL. Dr. A. W. Chapple, addressed a gathering of day school teachers in the arts theatre of the Liverpool University. on Saturday afternoon. The treating was under the auspices of the United Kingdom Band of Hope Union, whose autumnal conference in Liverpool has just closed. The lecturer said be had ceased to consider drunkenness at all. It was universally condemned even by brewers, who had found that drunkenness did not pay them. The question was, could they condemn the habit of drink- ing as they condemned drunkenness ? Ile believed they could. The man who was drinking daily without becoming anywhere near drunkenness was in more danger than the man who became drunk regularly at week ends, for the latter gave his system a chance to get rid of the alcohol, while the other was continually bathing his tissues in poison. There was no single factor known to science which so weakened cell tissue In disease as alcohol, and in certain diseases the chance of a rnsn's recovery was dependent entirely upon the amount of alcohol he had consumed—the smaller the amount the better was his chance.
