WOMEN'S GHAT.

The Prince of Wales.—Several of the Royal palaces are being cleaned,re-decorated,and modernised. Exten- sive operations of this kind are in hand at Buckingham Palace, St James' Palace, and at Frogmore House, near Windsor, which, it is said on good authority will be taken over by the Prince of Wales, on the completion of his year at Oxford. The Prince, as an undergraduate of Magdalen College, will join his fellow students for the Michaelmas term which begins on October 11th and will occupy a smite of rooms in the picturesque quad- rangle. His surroundings will be of the pleasantest,for the College with its 15th century chapel, hall, and cloisters, is beautifully situated and its great tower is the glory of Oxford. Magdalen ranks with Christ- church as one of the most exclusisre and aristocratic of the Colleges of Oxford. Each has its distinctive characteristics. Reble is eccleslaatic, Trinity is athletic, Balliol is highly intellectual, and also strongly anti- woman suffragist. To Magdalen, men mostly go to work, not to idle, and it is less given, than some of the colleges,to that horseplay which undergraduates usually regard as part and parcel of their University career. On Vegetables.—The Universal Cookery and Food Association are undertaking a series of lectures on the importance of vegetables in overy day diet ire, a fact not usually sufficiently appreciated in the middle class kitchen. The onion, for instance, both raw and cooked, has many virtues, not only from a dietetic but from a medicinal point of view ' • it is foodi for skin disetses, and boiled with milk and eaten the last thing at night, is, for many people, a cure for sleeplessness. Carrots also are excellent for the complexion, especially if eaten raw as may be done with the small young roots. Potatoes, we are told, contain their chief nutritive con- stituents just under the skin, and these are often lost by careless peeling. Much too, is said in favour of mushrooms, which ire very plentiful this year. There are four or flee edible kinds, commonly found in this country, and in order that they may be easily distin- guished from poisonnua fungi, the Board of Agriculture has published an cfficial booklet, giving dessriptions and illustrations, which may be had for Is. from th Secretary, 4, Whitehall Place, London, Frugal Living and Good tlealth.—That people can live on threepence a day is frequently shown by the wonderful things in the way of frugal living accom- plished by rural cottagers on scanty means. A typical example is afforded by an aged couple of Orsett in Essex, who hroe just celebrated their golden wedding. They commenced married life on Bs. 6d.aweek, never had more than 121. a week, and have brought up a family of eleven on these slender resources. It is such people as these that Sydney Smith had in mind when he calcu- lated how much more food and drink he had consumed than was necessary for health and strength. He found that this excess between 10 and 70 years of years of age would make 44 waggon loads, costing in all about £7,000 and he estimated that his voracity had becn equiva- lent to starving to death at least ICO persons. In the end he discovered that the secret of good health in old age was to eat and drink about one-bait of what you could eat and drink, advice which the middle-aged very often do not take sufficiently to heart. Women in Trousers.—The women workers on Major Whitmore's tobacco farm at Methwold, Norfolk, wear a bifurcated garment, in order to prevent ir•jury to the plants by ekirts. A similar style of dress is worn by the women engaged in the cockle gathering industry at Stiffkey, also In Norfolk. Mrs Bsrlyon in "Sunrise- land" has told of the hard life of these cockle gatherers. They wade in the water, knee deep or more, but the coast is treacherous, and it is unsafe for them to work, except in gangs, and sooner or later it seems, rheumatism is the inevitable lot of them all. Black Furs Pepular.—The choice of black furs is a wide one, for they are greatly in demand. Black fox is perhaps the most beautiful of these, but it becomes every year rarer, oonsrquently more expensive, aid black wolf makes a very good substitute,roore especially in those stranded effects to which this pelt lends itself admirably. It needs an expert to distinguish many of this year's wolf sets from bl ck fox, for the skins are dressed with a soft new Reis's, and are wonderfully supple. Black bear sets have much to rec3rnmend them • they are smart in appearance, wear well, and have the further advantage of being moderate in price. Isabella bear is worked in a variety of novel ways this autumn—and will doubtless attract many patrons. Skunk is very much in favour especially with ermine. Astral an is this year dressed to resemble broadtail rather than the fain,liir appearance it has worn so long. Fur and Spangles.—Fur and spangles are often employed on the same dress, and may be said to typify two leading forms of ornamentation. Some evening dresses are banded horizontally with fur, ermine being first favourite for this mode of trimming. In some instances where the dress is pure white, the coldness is relieved by small wreaths of flowers, passed round the fur at Intervals. A white tulle evening gown was thus decorated. Gauzy materials, which are too fragile to bear fur, are ornamented with feather trimming. Fringes of beads finish tunics that are almost covered with these decorations, and silk fringe is largely used In connection with imitation furs, of which sets, con- sting ss)les iii m an, les very in lei in evidence.

The Employment of Different Fabrics.—There le 6 decided tendency to employ a variety of materials OP the same gown which, when the different fabrics hav,e_ been judiciously chosen, and carefully arranged, 16 decidedly satisfactory—moreover it makes for econoreY: An afternoon frock for early autumn wear, is of fee! different materials.—The clinging white silk skirt 1)53 blue ninon draperies, whilst the coatee is of taffetas in e blue and white chessboard check, the Directoiro collar and cuffs being of black velvet. Two shades of ece colour are often most successfully combined,and brow° in this connection, is for the moment in high fsveur with dresamakers and desigoers. Adjustable Skirt Widtbs.—There will be less enthtle" iasm for serge this season than we have seen for the past year or two. The model houses are anticipslil a return to the accordion-pleated skirt, and in tu form no material makes up better than cashmere, whico has many points in its favour as a dress material. Sorc , of the new tailor-mades are showing accordion-pleaten basques, and walking suits are allowed a little slier!: width in skirts. A new idea is to have the skirt title" with adjustable sets of side pleats, which can be woret either buttoned up, or by the release of a straP, se. free to their full extent. Some French women ar' wearing skirts with side openings, but in the forccil mentioned, the notion is a novel and practical one, enid in the present stage of this important detail, it wee,!, not be surprising to see it exploited very generally c, the ready-to-wear costume ho uses. Embroidery for Autumn Mits.—Gies with clever nimble fingers, may trim their hats of beaver, plush, er fur, smartly, originally, and very inexpensivelY bd; means of flower embroidery in 'has silks, which ma! LYM M.

INSURANCE AND FRIENDLY S OCTET] e Nationa Insurance Act was the cause of another Friendly Society meeting being held in Lymm. This time it was the Lymm Female Benefit Society, an association operating in the parishes of Bollington. Grappenhall, Lymto, Oughtrington, Thelwall, Warburton, and the township of High Legb, who held a meeting in the National school on Monday night in order to decide what p^sition the society would take under the above Act.—After discussion, the members decided to unite with the Cheshire R.W.F.S.

CONCERT IN THE DRILL HALL.—Mr Carter of North- wich, brought a party of vocal and instrumental artistes over to Lymm on Wednesday night and gave an excellent evening's entertainment in the Drill Hall. The audience showed their appreciation of every item by unstinted applause. The poor attendance was no doubt due to the belief that the party was an unknown quantity, but of its excellent quality we can now vouch for, our Lymm representative stating that for sheer merit, the concert was the best miscellaneous one that has been held in Lymm for a long time. The programme was opened by Mr It Pickering, of the Chester Cathedral Choir, whose tenor voice was heard to advantage in " Quite " and later on "Island of Civil," the latter piece obtaining for the vocalist a well earned encore. Miss Deakin, winner of the first prize for a contralto solo at the Chester Eisteddfod, was also in good form, her rendering of "My Dear Soul " necessitating an encore. At another stage of the concert she sang" Annie Laurie "and as an encore "Coming thro' the Rye," which in its turn captivated the audience. She also gave "The Rose Garden.' Mr E Gammond, a bass singer, was also to have taken part, but was unfortunately too ill to do so. Instru- mental music formed other items on the programme Mr T G Gammond gave clever solos on the 'cello entitled" Noreen Moreen "and " Sarabande," whilst. those who cared for cornet solos were well entertained by Mr E G Wood with "The Better Land," which was encored, and Squilli-la-Tromba et "Se-la-Stranchezza." Another choice item was the musical trio, A Barcarolle, by Messrs Dale, Hideo and Gammond, whilst the most popular item was the brilliant violin performance by Master Don Hiden, the boy prodigy, whose solos "Ist Fantasia " and "Le Canaria" were both enthusi- astically received,an encore being demanded in each case. The humorous element was amply provided for by Mr Donald Grant, whose character efforts in " Send for a policeman "and "Hang out the front door key " were warmly applauded, and Mr Jock Kenyon who appeared in Scotch costume and gave the song " Roamin' in the gloamin'," together with the necessary patter for which he was encored. At a later period he gave a graphio representation of an excited Scotchman at a football match which well merited the encore given. The duties of accompanist were well performed by Mr S Hale.

CHILDREN'S UNION SALE OF WORK. —One of the most noteworthy agencies of the Lymm Parish Church is the Children's Union and King's Messengers, a society which consists of about 160 members, who meet at the Lymm and Statham Day schools dnring the winter evenings. The first portion of the meeting is devoted to a Missionary Chat by the Rector (the Rev G B Thurston, M.A.) When this is finished the children are taken in hand by several local laaies who instruct them in domestic arts and crafts. The mem- bers were as busy as ever last winter and on Thursday afternoon an imposing exhibiton and sale of work was held in the Drill Hall which had been decorated with flags for the occasion. The work, which had been tastefully arranged on several stalls looked both neat and useful and reflected great credit on both the teachers and children. There was a large and enthusiastic audience all afternoon and before 6 o'clock, the time when the exhibition closed, the stalls were well cleared showing that a good sale had ensued, the proceeds of which are to be divided between the Waifs and Strays Society and Foreign Missionary Funds. Music was discoursed by Mr C S Tonk's quadrille band during the afternoon, —The Rector expounded the benefits of the Society which is an approved society under the Insurance Act. The following is a list of the stalls and teachers of the I work :—Wood-carving, brass and copper work: Miss E Dalton and Miss M Hatton, with Miss Duncan as helper Brass work : Miss M Morris and Miss Hankinson. Needlework: Miss E Callender, Miss Goflin, Miss Hill, and Miss Saville. Fancy work: Mrs Cooke and Miss Bosch. Stallhelper Miss Twist. Statham stall : Teachers of the girls : Miss It Dalton, Miss Morton and Mrs Watson. Teachers of boys : Miss Daleon, Miss F Dalton, Miss Toulmin,and Miss Vright. Small children's hanawork : Miss F Hatton, and Miss Wash- ington. Stripwork : Mr R Oddie, Flower fruit and plant stall : The Misses Callender. Refreshments: Mrs Burrows, Mrs New marsh, Mrs Watson and Miss Norris. Shooting range : Mrs Ditchfield and Mr Christian. Fancy bread stall : Mrs Gregson. Pump and fish pond : Miss M Kirk others. Competitions consisting of guessing the circumference of a plate, guessing the number ot sweets in a beg, hoop-la, tiddley-wink, etc. were attended to by the Grammar School girls. In the evening Tonk's band Mei in attendance for dancing which was largely indulged in by the young people.

LOCAL_ HARVEST FESTIVALS.

The nrimbers of the local churches are apparently uninfluenced by the opinions of those persons who look with disfavour on the holding of thanksgiving services this autumn, for on Sunday last Harvest Festival ser- vices were held at three pli.ces of worship, two have already taken place and several more are planned. The Parish Church Mission, which is held in the Statham Day School, conducted its service on Sunaay night. The room was tastefully decorated for the occasion, there being an abundance of flowers. The preacher was Mr Henry Stanton, of Thelwall, whose efforts so pleased his congregation at the harvest festival last year that his services were requested for the present occurrence. There was a good attendance and the collection amounted to £3 4s 6d. The floral cross which is an annual feature was this year sent to be placed on the grave of the late Harold Dixon who was concocted with the Mission up to the time of his death a few months ago. The Congregational Church held their sErvices in the morning and evening. The Rev Mr. Williams of Tonga was the preacher and he chose as the basis of his remarks at the evening service the 15th chapter of St Luke, when he dwelt on the parables of the lost sheep, the lost piece of silver, and the prodigal son. At this service the children gave by desire a repetition of the hymm they rendered in the morning, "How loving God must be." The choir gave a spirited interpretation of the anthem "How excellent are Thy Mercies 0' Lord," Mr. J. Ellis taking the solo. Mr. A. Leather ably filled the position of organist and choirmaster. The thanksgiving services of the Oughtrington St. Peter's Church, which were commenced on Thursday, the 26th inst., when the Rev Canon Willis, Rector of Warrington, was the preacher, were continued on Sunday. The sacred edifice which was decorated inside with dowers, fruit and corn looked very pretty. The font decorations were the work of Mr and Mrs Sam Davies, whilst Mrs and Miss Schmidt confined their attentions to the pulpit and screen, and Mrs Wethered did the chancel end. The workers of the parish decorated the gas brackets, &o. The preacher was the Rev Canon °nerd. vice-president of St Edmund's Hall Oxford, whose discourse was greatly appreciated Mr 0 o:Smith presided at the organ with his usual skill. Offertories for the Church expenses. were taken up, there being an urgent need of funds owing to the recent repairs.

ULSTER'S REAL RIFLES. AN AMAZING SPEECH BY MR. KEBTY-FLETCHER. Mr. J. R. Kebty-Fletcher, M.P. for the Altrincbam Division, on Saturday opened a new Conservative club at Broadheath. With the exception of Altrincham Show, this was his first appearance in public since his long illness. He was warmly congratulated upon his recovery. Referring to Ulster, Mr Kebty-Fletcher said a wooden rifle was as good as any other for purposes of drill and to march up the street with. The reason the men of Ulster bad not got real rifles at the present time was that if they had a hundred thousand stacked the lot would be taken away, and that would not do. But the men of Ulster knew where the rifles were— from whence they were coming, most of them were not in Ulster. But according to his information, which came from the Lord Mayor of Belfast, audit really was public property, they had got revolvers. Revolvers were no use in a scrap in war; they might as well have a pea-shooter. But if the men of Ulster marched south on Dublin they would be armed and armed adequately. Let them go to America end see how people loathed and detested the Irish politicians there. The Irish politician there lived on the white slave business. That was largely how Tammany had come up. Were the

===== 0002601_19121005_art0125.txt | included=true | included =====
The Examiner, Saturday, October 5, 1912.

men of Ulster going to allow their country to be governed in the same way, as it would be if it was under the control of the Nationalists? The Ulstermen had the right to defend themselves. He was dining the other night with one of the most prominent politicians who was taking part in this campaign, and the question arose as to who Ulster was going to fight. The Nationalists were very strong when it came to cutting tails off cows in the middle of the night. (Yes.) If a Unionist farmer had a good horse, they were great at cutting its ears off. They were great at murder. If two men like Cavendish and Burke were walking in the park, they were brave when they stabbed them. With dynamite they were very brave. There had been a case in Man- chester. They were good at shooting policemen and good with dynamite. Why could they not say "We will go north to Ulster. These men have only got wooden rifles ?"—(Laughter.) Why could they not say "We will go north and take Ulster? " In the House of Commons and at the hustings they were told that the Trish Nationalists over-whelmingly represented Ireland. If they were ten to one, with all these Irish from America coming over, why could they not take those farmers in the north who only had wooden rifles? In the wilds of county Cork, and in the south, when it came to assassinating or cutting off tails or shooting through windows, they were good at that, but when it came to facing men who were armed' and drilled then nothing further was heard. It behoved the Radical party to go very cannily in this matter. Mr F E Smith had said that if the farmers of Ulster were shot down there would be lynching in the streets of London. They could not dictate to a successfsl army, and if anything happened there was no doubt there would be no State trials. In the twentieth century that would never do. State trials lasted years and years and nothing came in the end. But if the present Government had men shot down in Ireland there would be a drumhead court-martial, and they knew what happened—a man made premature acquaintance with his Maker. There was no State trial ; it would have to be short and sudden, because he was confident that the British people would not stand their own flesh and blood being shot down ; and he was confident that if there was a civil war in Ireland the North would win and win easily. (Loud applause).

GARDEN SOCIELIPS AUTUMN SHOW:.

The Church Room, Victoria-square, Stockton Heath, presented a gay and inviting appearance on Saturday, when the annual autumn chrysanthemum show of the Stockton Heath Garden Society was held. Those who have strolled through the village recently have been full of admiration for the beautifully laid-out gardens which have looked at their best with all the well-blending colours of the crhysantherrum blooms. When the best of these flowers have been cut and artistically arranged, and staged in a room for show purposes, one can readily imagine the striking and pleasing result which accrues. Such was the scene which met one on enter- ing the Church Room on Saturday, and the judges Messrs Goves (Walton Hall) and Wilkinson (Walton Lea) must have experienced considerable difficulty in awarding the prizes, for to the uninitiated there did not appear much to choose between each exhibit. More competitors exhibited at the show than ever before and the entries were twenty in excess of last year. The judges stated that the quality of the exhibits was superior to that seen during the last two years, and, taking into account the very wet season, it spoke volumes for the amount of trouble to which the exhibitors must have gone. The features of the show were the sprays of chrysanthemums and the dahlias, which it would have been difficult to excel in quality. The baskets of autumn berries in the junior section testified to the interest which the children are taking in the society. Several fine exhibits which were not for competition were sent by the following and were a source of admiration to all :—A beautiful display of chrysanthemums, celosias and ferns from Walton Hall, Mr Jas Ryan, Stockton Heath, Mr G Tandy, Latchford, Mr John Robson, Altrincham, and Mr William Treseder, Cardiff. Selections by the Garden Society's orchestra, under the able conductorship of Mr W E Ray, were greatly appreciated and they were accorded a hearty vote of thanks for their service at the close of the show. OPENING CEREMONY. Mr W H Woodcock (chairman of the Society) per- formed the opening ceremony and he was supported by a large number of officials including Mr White (Hon Secretary), and Mr D E Watson (Hon Treasurer). Mr Woodcock said the Garden Society was progress- ing rapidly, for they now had a membership of about 700 and it was their ambition to increase that to at least a thousand. The interest taken in the juvenile section greatly pleased the Committee for a great deal of labour had been spent on that section and it was on that department that they largely relied for the future development of the Society. They had had a very successful year, for they had not only increased their membership, but for the first time in their history they had been able to complete the year's work with a substantial balance at the .bank, and that was very gratifying. (Applause.) During the winter they proposed to have occasional lectures on the work of the society. He had never seen one of their autumn shows so nicely arranged and with such splendid exhibits as the one that day. As chairman of the society, he expressed his thanks to the hon. secretary (Mr White), Mr Watson (the hon. treasurer) and Mr J H Fletcher (the deputy-chairman), and all the members of the committee for the valuable assistance they bad rendered to the society. (Applause). On the proposition of Mr Watson, seconded by Mr Gorton, a vote of thanks was accorded to the judges, and Mr Wilkinson replied. A vote of thanks was accorded to the Chairman on the proposition of Mr H B Houghton seconded by Mr F A Jenkins and Mr Woodcock replied.

NO CHANGE FOR THREE YEARS. Earles/own Incident.

A RECHABITE MAJORITY.

WARRINGTON JUVENILE DISTRICTS'

COMING OF AGE.

The inauguration of the Temperance Movement, was responsible for one of the greatest and best crusades against the pernicious system which existed of friendly societies' holding their meetings in public houses,and it soon became manifest to the early I Temperance workers that Friendly or thrift societies must be sepirated from the drink shop.ln 1835 a Total Abstinence Friendly Society was instituted in Salford and was named the Independent Order of flechabites the name being suggested by the striking incident recorded in the 35th Chapter of Jeremiah. The Rechabite Order has always been, and still continues, in the van of progress and efficiency, as the increas- ing membership and accumulated funds plainly illustrate. Branches were formed in all parts, and in 1891 the Warrington Juvenile District No. 4 was formed, and since its inception has made rapid strides. The progress which has been made from the first has been under review during the week, for the society have been holding the coming-of-age celebrations. The Committee's report, which is included in a hand- some souvenir along with other interesting items, states that at the beginning of the year 1891 there were seven adult Tents with a membership of just over 390. As the adult sections were beginning to arouse themselves a little, they thought it was advisable to form a Juvenile District with a District Funeral Fund, and with that object in view a committee was elected, and after holding several meetings the Warrington Juvenile District was duly instituted on September 28th, 1891, at Bryn near Wigan. The report then deals fully with the pro- gress made by the society and states that since January, 1892, up to the present time 455 members had been transferred to the adult tents. They had also paid out in funeral claims on the deaths of 100 members £4OS 16s. and over ..€550 was fairly invested. The membership at the end of June 50th was 1,434 comprising 20 Juvenile Tents. The Juvenile District is worked apart from the Adult District and has its own officers. In conclusion the Committee sincerely hoped that all brethren and sisters would endeavour to do their best to push forward their Rechabite principles and never cease to work until every boy and girl had been secured for membership in the Order.

The celebrations during the week have taken the form of public meetings and social evenings in the various parts of the district, and commenced with a huge demonstration at Ashton-in-Makerfleld on Saturday last, followed by a public meeting in the Congrega- tional schools Meetings have also been held at Orrell, Haydock, Downall Green, Bryn, Lowton St. Mary's, and in Warrington at the Wycliffe Memorial Hall, Brick- street Mission Hall, %Vest-street Mission Hall, Friars' Green schoolroom, Thewl is-street schoolroom, Wesleyan schoolroom, Stockton Heath, Wesleyan schoolroom, Padgate, and Wesleyan schoolroom, Penketh. The speakers have been Bros R Bell, H.S.J.T., S A Scarlett. Jfi Lear Caton, and A Tomlinson. Capital musical programmes have been submitted at all the meetings and the celebrations which terminate to-day, have been a huge success. MEETING IN WYCLIFFE 'MEMORIAL HALL.

There was a fair attendance in the Wycliffe Memorial Hall, Warrington, on Wednesday evening when an enthusiastic meeting was held. Mr J Upson presided and the speaker was Bro A Tomlinson. The banner which the Wycliffe Tent 2214 received for securing the highest number of new members during the nine months ending June was placed on the platform. The CHAIRMAN was pleased to have a share in those perfectly justifiable rejoicings by which they were celebrating the attainment of the majority in the Juvenile Teats of the district. It was very satisfactory to know that the Juvenile Tents had made such rapid progress in the comparatively short period of 21 years. They had now passed through the penniless ages ,of infancy, boyhood, and adolescence, and the district had now, reached the age of energetic manhood and that night they were celebrating the fact. It spoke well for the energy,self denial and sacrifice and skill of the pioneers of the movement and those who stood by the juvenile tents in their penniless period, infancy and youth. He thought that whilst they were celebrating the attainment of their majority and taking a retrospect of the past, whilst looking back on the way they bad come, and rejoicing in the achievements they had made, at the same time be thought they ought to take a prospective view of the position. He was quite sure the officers in that district did not intend to rest on the laurels they had won but that they would look on the success they had a4iieved and take from that a stimulus for the future.lt Was no small achievement to have gained 1434 daring the time the district had existed. There was however, an immense field before them which was at present untouched. (Hear, hear.) He thought he was well within the limit when he said that there were 20,000 children in the county borough of Warrington who were eligible for the Juvenile Tents. Probably they would not be able to get the majority of those children in, but he thought they ought to aim at getting 5,000 or 6,000 of the children of Warrington in their tents. That might seem a large ideal, but large ideals would only bring about large achievements. (Hear, hear.) Dealing with the need to get the children in the Rechabite order as soon as ever possible,the speaker said it was a far easier matter to prevent youths froml falling away than it was to rescue those who had fallen. They wanted to instil in the hearts and minds of the younger generation the ideals of thrift and temperance, I and then they would be well on the way to securing respectable citizenship They wanted to push forward their good principles so that it would be made possible for the children who were living in darkness to come out of the gloom and evil, and fled their way into the light of freedom and happiness. (Applause). They deserved every encouragement in their noble cause, and those celebrations would give them added stimulus to go on to greater accomplishments. ;Applause). Bro. R. BELL (Middlesborough) then gave an interesting and instructive address on the objects of the movement and dealt with their position as an approved society under the Insurance Act. A mnsical programme was ably disposed of by the Bewsey Male Voice Choir (Mr W Wright, conductor), Misses F Watson and Ada Hamer. Miss L Turton ably accoinpanied the artistes and at the close a comprehen- sive vote of thanks was heartily accorded all those who had taken part in the meeting, on the proposition of Bro. B Walker seconded by Bro W Wakefield. The celebrations will close to-day (Saturday) with a conference of Juvenile superintendents and workers in Bold-street schools, Warrington, at 2.30 p.m., when a paper will be read by the High Superintendent, Bro It Bell. The following members of the Board will take part :—Bros S A Scarlett, J H Lear Caton, and A Tomlinson. After tea in Winmarleigh Cafe a public meeting will be held in the Bold-street schools when Mrs Dodds will preside.

ST. HELENS.

SANATORIUM EXTENSION.

The St. Helens Parliamentary Committee has agreed to purchase land adjoining the Sanatorium at Peasley Cross for the purpose of extensions. For some years the Health Committee has treated consumptive patients whenever possible, at the sanatorium, and until a fully- equipped sanatorium is erected in a suitable locality this work will be carried on at Peasley Cross. SCHOLASTIC ST. HELENS. SATURDAY AFTERNOON CLASSES FOR MINERS Mr J W Yates, M.Sc, the chemistry and science master of Cowley Schools, St Helens, having secured a position at Cardiff, the St Helens Higher Education Committee has appointed Mr W Ritchings, of Burnley, to the vacancy. For the Technical School at the Gamble Institute Mrs M It Collins, St Helens, has been appointed teacher of French ; Mr H E Evans, Man- chester, teacher of commercial subjects, and Mr C L Patrick, Lewes.teacher of engineering. The committee has arranged a Saturday afternoon class for miners, and has arrtmged to become an examining body for shot- firers in mines. The Local Government Board has sanctioned a loan of £l6poo for the erection of a new Council school in Liverpool-road. RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

ELECTIVE AUDITORS. SEVEN CANDIDATES AT WIDNES.

At Widnes, on Tuesday, polling took place for the election of two elective auditors for the ensuing twelve months. As on previous occasions, the election was of the keenest order, and on Tuesday seven candidates offered themselves. The retiring auditors were F Martley and H Ackerly, the former of whom did not seek re-election. _ _

Shortly after eight o'clock the result of the poll was announced as follows :—A Moore, commercial clerk, 331 ; H Ince, insurance superintendent,3ls ; H Ackerly, signalman, 137 ; T Ashley, stonemason, 95 ; J H Faulkner, commercial clerk, 76 ; G Lowis, insurance agent, 57 ; T Cafferty, chfmical labourer, 40. The first two were elected. POLICE AND PUBLICANS. WIDNES OFFICER'S REPLY TO CRITICISM.

At the Widnes Police Station, on Saturday, Superin- tendent R. Strickland, upon his retirement from the Lancashire County Constabulary after forty-one years' service, was presented with a case of cutlery, a pipe, and a gold brooch for Mrs. Strickland, gifts from the members of the force. The Mayor (Alderman F. Neil) ' made the presentation, and eulogised the recipient's thirty-three years' work in Widnes. The Superintendent, in reply,thanked all the members of the force for their loyalty to him and for their kindness in thinking of him upon his retirement. Recently he had had the hardest nut of his life to crack, and this had caused a feeling in his mind which would not allow him to adequately express himself. He was finishing his duty, and he would like to refer to the remarks of a certain gentleman who had attacked the force,kuowing that they could not reply themselves. Everything.that had been said was a confounded lie. It was actually said that the police hobnobbed with publicans, and where policemen were publicans were there also. The men under his charge were respectable, honest and straightforward, and in his opinion and long experience there was not a more respectable body of men in the town than the publicans. They did their duty in a proper manner, as his. yearly statistics bad shown. Drunkenness had diminished and there was little crime of a serious nature.

===== 0002601_19121005_art0133.txt | included=true | included =====
OBJECTIONS NOT VALID.

IMPORTANT DECISION AFFECTING RUNCORN

vorEns.

Mr. A. Colt-Williams, revising barrister, gave an important decision at Runcorn Revision Court on Mon- day concerning the posting of notices of objection to voters by party agents. The Conservative agent (Mr. J. Edmondson) claimed that the section dealing with the posting of notices had not been compiled with properly by the Libor.%ls. Mr. Ogden (Liberal agent) said they had been enclosed in envelopes, which were handed over to the Postmaster of Nantwich. He admitted that there was no address on the duplicate notices nor were the notices addressed on the back. He claimed that there was nothing in the statute requiring the addresses to be so affixed, if envelopes were used. The Revising Barrister said the section laid it down clearly that the address of the individual for whom the notice was intended should figure on both the original and the duplicate notice. He found the objections as sent out were not valid.

A considerable number of votes in the Northwich Division are affected by the decision.

VYRNWY WATER IN RUNCORN WHISKY. UNAPPRECIATIVE CONSUMERS.

At the Runcorn Urban Council meeting on Tuesday night Mr Pringle mentioned that an engine at the waterworks had broken down at the moment that the other engine was being replaced. As a consequence Runcorn had had a supply of pure Vyrnwy water for four or five days. (Hear, hear.) As a result he had been repeatedly asked what was the matter with Runcorn water. People complained that it did not make good tea, and would not, dilute whisky properly. (Loud laughter.) Runcorn people bad been used to consuming a certain amount of soap, and they now complained that they got too much lather. If the people liked to retain the Vyrnwy water, they could do so at a cost of about aixnence on the rates. A proposal to borrow £B7O for the purpose of widen- ing Moughland-lane was opposed by Mr H ,Baker, who urged that it was a waste of money. The Chairman : The principle has already been accepted. It is only a matter of obtaining the money.

LANCASHIRE COTTON COMBINE. News was received on Wednesday in Liverpool that negotiations for the formation of a combine in the Lancashire cotton industry were concluded on Tuesday in Paris between a syndicate of French financiers and the directors of floe Lancashire firms. The combine will have a capital of a million and a half. FAREWELL TO 150 MISSIONARIES. trader the auspices of the Church Missionary Society a meeting was held at the Royal Albert Hall on Wednesday night, when a large audience bade a cordial farewell to 150 missionaries who were leaving for the Far East. Bishop Price, of Fukien, said the more missionaries went to the interior of China the more pleased would be the Republican Government.

, WOMAN MADE HAPPY.

If by your knowledge, patience and skill, you could not only make all your women friends happy, but carry peace and relief into thousands of homes all over the world, would you not be a great benefactress to your sex? This is what a woman has done. By reading her message you may yourself benefit by her good work and carry it on by passing the knowledge to others. This message, which relates to the suffer- ings of womanhood, is sent to women only, and in a plain sealed envelope. It is called Lydia E. Pink- ham's Text Book for Women, and may be had free of all charge by writing to the Lady Manager, c!o The Lydia E. Pinkham Medicine Co., 42a, Hampstead Road, London, N.W. Mrs. Pinkham was enabled to relieve the suffering of her fellow women by a herbal mixture now known all over the world as Lydia E. Pinkham's Vegetable Compound. It was because she could not, by her personal efforts, send it to all in need, that Mrs. Pinkham put it into the hands of chemists, by whom it is sold at 2s. 9d., or for a much larger bottle 4s. 6d. (The Lady Manager will send it post free at the same prices, if desired.) What Lydia E. Pinkham's Vegetable Compound does for women may be put into a sentence. It relieves all the suffering which afflict women by reason of their sex. Every dose carries relief, relaxation and soothing peace to thl female organisation, and it is equally efficacious for women of all ages and conditions.

The Musical Society. you kindly allow me to call the Wen-, tion of your readers to the Musical Society's ad- vertisement in this issue, and to rerdind them that , the societys season commences on October 7th, with the performance of A. Cellier's opera "Dorothy." Much time, trouble and expense has been incurred to, mate the production Worthy of the society, and if possible superior to any previ- ous one. The opera is a very pleasing one, and the introduction of a pack of fox hounds gives a novel touch to the piece. Turning now to the society's musical pro- gramme, there will be three subscription concerts as usual, which will be of a particularly pleasing nature. The music will include works by well- known compoSers. viz. : Stanford's "Pbadryg Crohoore," Coleridge-Taylor's "A Tale of Old . Japan," Hadyn's "Creation." Tschaikowsky 's 'Violin Concerto, and several short works for both chorus and orchestra.' • ' The professional engagements will include 'Madame Marie Bremp bone of the finest soprano vocalists of the present day), Miss Nellie Ander- ton. Mr. John Booth, Mr. Hamilton Harris, Mr. R. Evans. and Mr. Naum Blinder, a violinist of exceptional ability and promise; and further en- , gagements are pending. Your readers will have seen your report of the society's annual meeting last week and the state- ment of accounts. It will be noted that these shew a serious falling off in he support given by sub- scribers. This is greatly to be deplored, seeing that the society does not work for profit finan- cially. but for the furtherance of musical taste and ability, and the good of the town generally. It has done in the past, and is still doing an ex- cellent work, and it really should be better sup- ported than is the case. But apart from any moral obligation to support the society, the excel- lence of the concerts, and the benefit and pleasure to be derived from them, should induce even the least musical to subscribe. Surely no better value could be given as matters stand, but were the number of subscribers increased, these concerts could not only be given without loss, but they could be rendered even more attractive by the engagement of the very highest professional talent obtainable. I would therefore strongly and ur- gently appeal to your readers and others to come forward and help the society in its work by be- coming subscribers.—,Yours sincerely. Jolts P. REYNOLDS. Hon. Treasurer.

NOVEMBER ELECTIONS.

INTERESTING CONTESTS CERTAIN.

There is a prospect of fights in practically every ward on November Ist—in fact six interesting con- tests are already assured. Mr Edmund Osborne has decided to stand as an Independent candidate in St Austin's Ward, where the retirng Conservative repre- sentative, Councillor Ashcroft, will probably seek re-election. 11Ir Neil t..-lone is also spoken of es a possible candidate. In Whitecross Ward, as we have already announced, Councillor Bellian (C) will be opposed by Mr Peter Peacock. chairman of the Executive Committee of the Warrington Liberal Associ- ation. Mr E Wynne Jones has consented to contest Latchford Ward in the Liberal interests against Conn. Wilson (C). In Fairfield Ward Councillor Jackson Holmes, the retiring Liberal member, %%ill have as his Conservative opponent, Mr T r Bradshaw. Coun- cillor Owen Toole (L) will again seek thci riffrages of the Bewsey Ward electors:and Mr A C B.nks end Mr Samuel Gough have announced their intention of tranding as Independents. In Orford Ward, the Conservatives are challenging the return of Councillor Cunnington (L), their candidate being Mr W Brad- bury. Councillor Sankey, the retiring Conservative representative of Howley Ward, will not seek re-election and Mr John Chrimes is standing in the Conservative interest. The Liberals are taking steps to contest the ward. _

MR. EDMUND OSBORNE

who is contesting St. Austin's Ward as an Independent candidate, was born in 1873 and is therefore 39 years of age. He was educated first at People's College and later at the Boleler Grammar School. On leaving school he went into the ((lice of Mr. Arthur Bennett, Chartered Accountant, with whom he spent seven years. latterly as confidential clerk. It was in December 1897 that Mr. Osborne entered the service of the Warrington Savings Bank where he now holds the position of cashier. He is a gentleman of many parts. He takes a keen interest in all matters affecting the welfare of the people and his ready pen has been used to further any good cause. He was largely instrumental in the formation of the Warrington Parliamentary Debating Society and it was from a letter of his in the local press that a discussion followed and the society was inaugurated. He believes that politics should not be introduced into the affairs of local self government and that candidates for municipal honours should be elected not because they are party men but because they are business men. He is a firm believer in athletics and has won many prizes himself as a " sprinter " in his younger days. For several years, he has been a member of Committee of the Warrington Football Club. This season he has been appointed Chairman of the Committee and the representative of the Club on the Lancashire County Football Union. The work for the amelioration of the lot of the poor children of the town has always appealed to him and he has been appointed Financial Secretary of the Warrington Ragged School and Mission. Mr Osborne is a gentleman well-versed in accounts, and his knowledge of affairs should be of great service to the ratepayers. He is the right type of representative and we welcome his candidature and hope that the electors of St. Austin's Ward will ieturn him to the Council, and by doing so they will not only get an earnest worker for the ward, but for the town at large.

Warrington Cricket, Bowling & fennis Club. This club brought the season to a close with a successful social on Saturday evening, -when 100 members sat down to tea in the Club Bouse. After- wards an enjoyable musical programme was submitted by Mrs Booth, Mrs Haviour, Miss Corney, and Messrs P C Price, T Critchley. J W Bibby, F Fieldhouse, George Smith, P 0 Bailey, Alf Silcock, II H Pearson, R T Fairclough, and W H J Jenkins. Mr W H Payton was the accompanist. The president's prizes for bowling were won by Mr R H Pearson and Mr E A 'relfer ; and for tennis by Miss Doris Parkinson and Mr Stanley Partridge, and they consisted of a hand- some timepiece and cases of silver.—A pleasing item in the proceedings was the presentation by the tennis members of a silver flower stand of chaste design to Mr and Mrs Johnson on the occasion of their marriage. Mr Johnson has been for some years the popular secretary of the tennis section of the club.— Mr. William Silecck, who presided, said the past season bad, on the while, been a fairly successful one. He congratulated the club upon the completion of the installation of the water supply, which was a great boon, and said that sooner or later they would have to take the lighting question into consideration.

Lovers of light opera are looking forward with considerable interest and pleasure to the performances of" Dorothy" which will be given at 64 Royal Court Theatre next week by the opera section of tbe Warrington Musical Society., The enterprise promises to be fully in keeping with the Society's high traditions and there is every reason to believe that another brilliant success will be added to an already lustrous record. The opera, which is on the good old!English lines of "Torn Jones," transp )rts us to the pictureeque surroundings of the mid 18th century peri-d.The idyllic story and lyrics provided by Mr B C Stephenton are wedded to Cellier's graceful and singularly appropriate music and the result is a work which has enjoyed a remarkable degree of popularity. The rehearsals have proceeded most satisfactorily,wi th Mr. F. H. Crossley and Mr. R. Walford Lomax again at their respective patts of conductor and stage manager, and the performances bid fair to realize the highest expectations. The cast is as follows : Dorothy Mrs. C. '' DOROTHY.

GARTON STONE.

machiees in the early part of the eighteenth century. " And what is the general tone d the opera and its music ?" " The prevailing characteristics "replied Mr Lomax "are taste and refinement. The librettist always pro- vides opportunity for these graceful numbers which are found in Mr. Cellier's work. The music precisely suits the words, and for the most par( the tuneful old English rhythm marks the verses. It is bright and melodious throughout and comprises some very pretty ballet movements, indeed, the whole of the dance music is catchy and taking, without being flippant." ` I suppose the scenic effects will be exceptionally plea-ing ?" " Yes, some delightful pictures of old English country life will be preset ted. The first act is a particularly attractive set —outside the Hop Pole Inn, the" Kentish village h, stelry. The second ,act is a massively built up scene,representiog the interior of an old English mansion, Chanticleer Hall. It is entirely of oak and comprises two handsome staircases and a gallery. This act ought to be particularly effective. When the guests, tired with the dance, retire to rest, we have all the excitement of an attempted robbery. The commotion subsides, the squire very magnani- mously offering to refund to the pseudo Duke of Bark- shire the money of which he is supposed to have been relieved, and the company are again about to seek reprse wheti the sout.d of the huntsmen, who are out cubbing, once more arouses their sporting spirit and the act closes with a very effective scene as the hunts- men and hounds enter the hall of Sir John Bantam's mansion. '1 he setting of the last act is a delightful bit of woooland painting —the Round Coppice. As regards the costumes, we are back again in the patches sod powder period. In the first and la3t acts we have some attractively dressed peasants, while in the second act— the country ball—there is the contrast of a magnificent , display of old satins and brocades. "

"A production of this kind must mean exact:ng work for everybody concerned?" "The position of a stage director is no sinecure. The artistic temperament is not made of cast iron and sometimes shows signs of wear and tear. They call me a 'coach ' down here—a word I don't like, as it is too suggestive of 'cramming.' I think I go much beyond that. I carefully explain to the per formers why a thing must be (I,,ne and then show them how to do it. Expressive movement is!just as necessary to the development of an opera as the spoken word or the music, but it is almost too much to expect an amateur corn cany to do in four wceks' rebearsal what professionals do in eight weeks. When amateurs succeed in giving a first-class performance, their untiring zeal and intelligence should have full acknowledgement. That the Warrington Musical Society's cperatic section will present a worthy performance I am confident.`Dorothy' will be quite up to the excellent standard they have set themselves in previous enterprises. Our principals are almost all well-tried favourites of the public and bid fair to add to their laurels. During the evening some particularly charming dances will be executed. In the first act there occurs characteristic peasant dance. The second act very properly opens with an old cbuntry measure which is succeeded by a stately minuet. In the last act, amid beautiful sylvan surroundings, we do an entire rustic ballet." Mr. Lomax pointed out that the scenery has been specially painted for this production. " The general staff at the theatre, under Mr. Loader's direction " he added "is determined to leave nothing undone by way of facilitating the success of the performances, and the acting Manager, Mr. 11. L. Warburton is quite enthusiastic in carrying out Mr. Loader's ideas. I may say that I am very happy to be associated for another year with the society's excellent conductor, my friend Mr. Crossley."

NEW CHESHIRE MAGISTRATES. Local Appointments.

Twenty-one names have been added to the Com- mission of the I'eace for the County of Cheater. They were recommended by the Lord Lieutenant of the county, the Duke of Westminster, on the advice of the Cuunty Advisory Committee, and have been approved by the Lord Chancellor. The majority of the recipients 11 of the honours are Liberals. The local appointments are :—Charles Jackson ' Holmes, Barnacre, Latchford Without, near War- rington, insurance broker (L.); John Alfred Ransome, Shirley Hill, Cliffe, near Warrington, manu- facturer (L,) ; Henry Gordon Roberts, Brentwood, Appleton, elate merchant, Captain 4th Battalion South Lancashire Regiment Territorials (L) ; John James Whitley, Hatton Cottage, Hatton, near War- rington, managing director' Greenall, Whitley and Co. Wilderepool Brewery, Warrington (C.) ; Edwin Woods, Rock Villa, Latchford, wire manufacturer (C). COUNCILLOR CHARLES JACKSON HOLMES

Of Barnacre Latchford Without is the only son of the late Mr. Charles John Holmes, for many years an Alderman of the Borough, the " father of the Town Council," and Mayor in the years 1863-1 and 18r)9-70. Councillor Jackson Holmes, who has been for many years closely identified with the educational and philanthropic life of the borough, was born at Friars Green, educated at the old Newton-street school (the headmaster at that time being the late Mr. Edward Brewtnall) the Friends' Sett ol at Penketh, and the Stramongate School, Kendal. For some time he assisted his father in his business as a woollen draper at Market Gate, after which he was connected with a Kendal firm, for whom he travelled for three years, since which time he has lived in Warrington, succeed• ing to the business at Market Gate on his father's decease, but relinquished it some years ago to become an insurance broker and an estate agent. He has been a member of the Town Council for three years as one of the representatives of the Fairfield Ward. He is also a member of the Education Com- mittee, and in this connection it is worthy of note that his wife is the only lady member of that body. Mr. Holmes is a many aided man, and has filled many public offices with a credit to himself and benefit to his native town. He is deputy chairman of the Savings Bank in Buttermarket-street, correspondent of and treasurer of the Arpley-street School (in which he manifests special interest), a Commissioner of Taxes for the Bucklow Unioa:treasurer of the Friends School at Penketh, is a Trustee and Life Governor of the ' Warrington Infirmary, for which be has acted as hon. auditor since 1904, and has filled the position of the clerk (president) of the Lancashire and Cheshire quarterly meeting of the Society of Friends. He is also the oldest member of the Society cf Fiends worshipping at the Friends' Meet:ng House in Buttermarket-street. Be was the first hon. secretary of the Warrington School of Science, which used to meet in the rooms over Arpley Railway Station, and which owes its estab- lishment to him and the late Mr. Edward Milner, whose valuable work in Warrington. is still remembered with gratefulness by the older inhabitants. Ire was made a magistrate for the borough in 1901 by the Duchy of Lancaster, and is most attentive to his duties. Mr. Holmes, who is a Liberal in politics. was married in Manchester in 1898 to Miss Sutton, a daughter of the late Mr Edward Sutton proprietor of the International Sugar Journal, and editor of Bradshaw's Continental Guide.

CAPTAIN H. 0. ROBERTS. Captain Henry Gordon Roberts is the eldest son of Councillor Henry Roberts, J.P., of Kingsley. Hill Cliffe, and is 35 years of age. He was educated first at People's College and latterly at the Boteler Grammar School. He , commenced business with his father in Bold-street, and is now the chairman and managing director of the Warrington Slate Co., Ltd. He joined the old Volunteers in February, 1900 as second lieutenant and was promoted to a captaincy in July 1902, having charge of F Company. Like his father, Captain Roberts is an ardent Liberal, and takes an active part in the work both in Warrington and the Knutsford Divisions. Ile is a vice-chairman of the Stockton Heath Liberal Association, chairman of St. Austin's Ward Committee, Warrington, and a vice- president of the Warrington Liberal Association. He is also chairman of one of the committees of the War- rington Citizens' Guild ot Help. He was married in 1901 to Miss Milling, daughter of the late Mr Henry Milling, of Summerville, Latchford.

MR. EDWIN WOODS. Mr Edwin Woods is a prominent figure in commercial and political affairs in Warrington. He is one of the leaders of the wire industry and invented the con- tinuous wire-dawing machine which has been of incalcuable benefit—in fact it has revolutionized the trade. Mr Woods founded the Longford Wire Company to work bis several patents and the success of the concern is very largely due to his enterprise and initiative. Mr Woods is a strong Conservative and.

Mr. Lomax has been happily associated with two of the opera section's most notable enterprises—" rorn Jones " and "San Toy "—and the brilliant artistic success of those performances was ample proof of the thoroughness, tact and skill which lie brought to bear upon the onerous duties of " rroducer." To. au Examiner" representative Mr. Lomax courteously pave his impressions of the latest operatic venture of the society.

J. Leslie, who transferred it to the Prince of Wales' Theatre and thence to the Lyric. It bad the phenomenal run of 931 performancee in London, and was subsequently : touted in the English provinces and the Colonies, securing a profit for Mr. Leslie and others of no less than 1.60,000. P. & K. ANNUAL MEETING.

CRITICISM OF THE INSURANCE ACT.

The 39th ordinary general meeting of the Pearson and Knowles Coal and Iron Co. Limited was bold •at the offices, Beweey-road, on Monday at noon, " for tht+ pur- pose of receiving end passing the accounts for the year ended June 30th, and for transacting the general busi- ness of the company." Mr J S Harmood-Banner, M.P., the Chairman of the Board of Directors, presided, and there were also present : Directors, Messrs John J Bleckly, Richard Brancker, W Peter Rylands and Herbert S tsleckly ; shareholders, Messrs Pde G Coghill, A S Bleckly, John Bleckly, jun., Wilfred J Bleckly, James Richardson, John Knowles, J C Eccles, Richard Greenhalgb, Captain Harwood-Banner' W J Wallington, J Pickles, C E Bunch, Thomas Atherton, William Chadwick, A H Burgess, Wallace Smith, George Bowden, A J Duckworth, T Truelove, J Mainwaring, Alfred Hulme, J H Jackson, W D Williams, and A Brotherton (secretary). CHAIRMAN"

CHAlts.._ .'S SPEECH. The report of the Directors has already appeared in our columns. The profits for the year amount to £78,141 3s led, and the balance brought forward to £l3 359 33 Bd. An interim dividend at the rate of six per cent per annum on the first and second preference shares and three per cent per annum on the ordinary shares was paid in March last for the half-year ending December 31st, 1911, amounting to £19,633 15s. The directors now recommend that a dividend be paid for the past half-year at the rate of sir per cent. Per annum on the first and second preference shares, and seven per cent, per annum on the ordinary shares, making with the interim dividend five per cent, for the seer, and a total distribution of £50,755 16s Bd. The directors further recommend that £12,393 10$ 8d be added to the reserve fund, raising it to £352,500 ; that the sum of £14,528 3s Bd, whic`a represents the expenditure on capital account for the year, be written off, leaving a balance of £13,822 16$ lid to be carried forward. The CHAIRMAN in proposing the adoption of the reports and balance sheet, made a feeling allusion to the late Sir H Sleekly, who he said joined the Board in 1887, after 15 years' services with the company." Be has been one of the pillars on which we have built up this great concern, and throughout the whole of his career he has devoted his time and attention most un- selfishly to your interest. As you know. ironworks have to live on terms of friendship with their neigh- bours, and Mr Bleckly's services as a diplomat and n'egotiator were in constant reouisition. Ile was trea- surer of the Iron and Steel Institute for many years, and was one of the most prominent figures at their annual gatherings. Now, as regards this year, no aoubt you have studied the figures well, and will have noticed with satisfaction that we still exhibit the same caution in dividing our resources and in maintaining the strength of our position. We have had many difficulties to contend with during the past year, particularly by the unsettlement of labour and the coal strike. It. is quite true that as regards

THE COAL STRIKE we have benefited by realizing all the coal we bad on stock, and the little prophecy I made last year, recom- mending every consumer and householder to fill up his stocks or cellars to the fullest capacity, was well timed, (Hear, hear.) I hope all of you, at any rate took full advantage of it, and did not suffer from shortness of coal during the strike. I did my hest Ito bridge over the differences which arose with the miners, but the strike apparently had to come, and the new principle of the minimum wage had to be adopted. The minimum wage imposes considerable labour on our staff ; in fact, we have had to increase it, but there were not the discrepancies to the same extent between the wages and demands of the men in our Lancashire district, and, where rates have been levelled up to the amounts we were al,eady I paying,we gained some advantage in sales by equalizing cost with our competitors. I sew, as regaras the figures, you will observe lon the capital and liabilities side that we have again strengthened our reserve fund. The dividend which we distribute will pay you five per cent, for the year, and absorb £50,755 of the profits earned. NoTv. the directors have thought it wise to call the share- holders' special attention to the heavy charges which the ordinary shareholders have to pay in taxes, rates, and workmen's compensation, and they do this in order that they may emphasise the further burden the ordinary shareholders have to bear under the working of THE NATIONAL .ussURANCE ACT, As stated, the effect of the Act represents a new tax of over one per cent per annum upon the amount of the ordinary capital, which is £610,000 ; in addition to which there will be heavy charges for extra clerks' time in order to I,eep up the immense amount of book work required. Now, I am not going to discuss the merits or demerits of the Act, or whether it will be advantageous generally to the nation or not. The

110==••••••

point which must appeal to all employers is the unfair and intquitable burden placed upon the industrial com- panies who employ large numbers of men, and have a heavy wages list. The burden is most inequitable. The average of profits is based to such a large extent upon the total cost of an article sold, and not upon the wages only of the employed, that when the value of the raw material is large, and wages small, the Insur- ance Act will fall much lighter than in those cases where the value of the raw material is low, and wages are high, and there the burden is very extreme. Now of course, no one will object to carrying out these schema if it is shown in any way how the amount which there schemes coat can be recovered, but so far I, at any rate, have not yet learned how this is to be dooe. In the coal trade it could be done, because we have not to meet competition elsewhere. Next year we shall he paying in taxes, rates, workmen's compensation and National Insurance OVER £40,000 A YEAR, and whilst, as some politicians have said, we have only to add it to the cost of the article and the amount charged disappears, yet my experience is that con- sumers are never too ready to pay increased costs; and unless times are bright, and demand is good, and competition disappears, the chance of getting back from your consumer the added cost of any article sold Is very slight. At any rate at the present time neither Great Britain nor its Government assist any industrial concern or individual to recoup the heavy charges which they so constantly impose. Moneys are lightly and airily spent and increase the popularity of the politician at very serious cost to the working population who have to pay for them. I saw in the paper the other day that the taxes per capita of Great Britain compare very unfavourably with the taxes of other nations. In the United Kingdom the charge is £3 4s Ild a head, against £3 2s Gd in France, £2 5s Gd in the Netherlands. £1 14s 9d in Germany, and £1 us 2cl in Belgium. Now most of these nations are ever on the watch to assist the industries of their country, but I do not think there is anyone who can give any particular instance of any assistance, in any shape or form, given by the Govern- ment of this country to those who do so much for its prosperity, namely, the industrial workers. As regards Partington, the strike first and the wet weather have delayed our progress, and we are not yet able to report the starting of either the coke ovens or the blast furnaces or the mills. I think, however, you will see a good start before next we meet, and those of you who are shareholders in the Partington Company will observe that we have called a meeting for this afternoon at the works in order that you may see the progress we have made there. I only wish we had been working at present. We should undoubtedly have shown an excellent result on the basis of the present trade. We went workmen's dwellings at Partington in order to provide easy access to our employees, of whom we shall have a good many. We were in negotiation at one time, and hoped to have gone forward with the schemes of building. These, however, had to be dropped in consequence of

THE LAND ACT OF 1909, and now we have to consider how we are going to bring our men backwards and forwards. There is no doubt about it whatever that the Act of 1909 interfered most materially with builders in pursuing their ordinary °muse of building, whereas in this case they were bound to have good customers for their land.

MR. J. J. BLECKLV AND THE COST OF _ _ _ THE STRIKE.

Mr. JOHN J. BLEci(Tr.:Ts;;;;;JdedTand said it would be in the memory of all present that his brother had been in the habit of seconding this resolution. He felt not only the loss of a life-long friend and companion, but he missed him in connection with the work of the company, of which be had taken his half-share and, while he welcomed the youhger men in coming to the assistance of their elders, he was afraid he could not look forward to anyone to fill his brother's place so long as he (the speaker) remained in the company. He was reminded of another fact which had a melancholy significance for him, and that was that he stood there as the lost of those eight men connected with the Board who were responsible for the launching of the company in 1873. They had all died in harness in the service of the company, except Mr. Beck, who retired some five years ago, and was still enjoying a well-earned rest. He could endorse what the Chairman had said in regard to the report and general legislation from which the Company suffered, and would add, if allowed to intro- duce the least tinge of politics.,that they were promised a feast of rare, refreshing fruit, but he thought if they compared the promise with the realization they would say it was the apples of .Sodom and gall and wormwood rather than what was originally described. MR. W. PETER RYLANDS AND THE INSURANCE Act

Mr. W. PETER RYLANDS seconded. In doing so he commented upon the burdens imposed upon the great producing companies, such as the Pearson and Knowles Coal and Iron Company, by taxes, rates, the Workmen's Compensation Act, and now, in addition, the National Insurance Act. It was a burden which, in the case of industries that were directly in competition with foreign manufacturers, could not be recovered from the cost. In the latter case the burdens bad either got to be borne by the workmen in a reduction of wages or to come from the pockets of the shareholders. No new company could start with any hope of success under existing conditions, and when investors could get four, five, or six per cult. on their money when invested abroad, without any anxiety whatever, and with absolute security, there was no inducement to put their money into such a perilous business as a great producing undertaking like that which receives all the kicks and blows of the community and very little encouragement indeed. The result was that as time went on their competitors who were not lit must fall by the way. The burdens to which reference bad been made could Lot be put on the rhoulders of the unfit, or If they were, the unfit could not survive. The Pearson and Knowles Coal and Iron Company would go on more and more efficiently, relying upon the support of the shareholders in pursuing the policy of setting on one side capital, in the form of a reserve fund to meet the burdens which the Government imposed upon them. If that policy was successful, and the hopes of the future were assured to the country, be believed with confidence they might look forward to the Pearson and Knowles Company standing out as a great landmark in the history of England for many years to come. ELECTION OF DIRECTORS. ' It was one of their great assets that they had him as a director. (Hear, hear, and applause.) Mr IiRANCKER seconded, and the motion was carried, Mr RYLANDS thanking the meeting for their renewed confidence in him.

The CHAIRMAN proposed the election of Mr John Mealy, jun. as a director of the company to act with his father. He was worthy of the position. Mr W PETER RI'LANDS seconded, and the motion was carried.

Mr JOHN BLECHLY, jun., said it would be his constant endeavotr to serve the company to the best of his ability, and he thanked the Chairman for the kind manner in which he had spoken of him. The CHAIRMAN, in acknowledgment, said all the directors were doing their best for the company. The new works at Psrtington, whilst they had been the cause of immense expenditure, were going to be one of the finest things in Lancashire. They were going to do grand things for Warrington in adding to the trade at the existing works, and they were very proud of every- thing connected with them. They were also very proud of the fact that the main erections at Partington had been carried out by this Company. (Applause).

HOUSING, TOWN PLANNING &c., ACT, 1909. WARRINGTON. WHEREAS the Mayor, Aldermen and Burgesses of the County Borough of Warrington have applied to the Local Government Board under Part II of the Housing, Town Planning &c., Act, 1909, for authority to prepare a town planning scheme with reference to certain lands situate within the said Borough, in regard to which notices of intention to make such application have been given in accordance with the Town Planning Procedure Regulations (England and Wales), 1910 : AND WHEREAS the Local Government Board have directed a local inquiry to be held into the subject matter of such application: NOTICE IS HEREBY GIVEN that Thomas Adams, Esq., who has been appointed to hold the Inquiry, will attend for that purpose at the Town Hall, Warrington, on Wednesday, the sixteenth day of October, 1912, at ten o'clock in the Forenoon and will then and there be prepared to receive the evidence of any persons inter- ested in the matter of the said Inquiry. Local Government Board, NOEL T. KERSHAW. 2nd October, 1012. Assistant Secretary.

THE EXAMINER• SATURDAY OCTOBER 5. 1912.

for its prosperity, namely, the industrial workers. As regards Partington, the strike first And the wet weather have delayed our prosresg, and we are not yet able to report the starting of either the coke ovens or the blast furnaces or the mills. I think, however, you will see a good start before next we meet, and those of you who are shareholders in the Partington Company will observe that we have called a meeting for this afternoon at the works in order that you may see the progress we have made there. I only wish we had been working at present. We should undoubtedly have Shown an excellent result on the basis of the present trade. We want workmen's dwellings at. Partington in order to provide easy access to our employees, of whom we shall have a good many. We were in negotiation at one time, and hoped to have gone forward with the schemes of building. These, however, had to be dropped in consequence of THE LAND ACT OF 1909, and now we have to consider how we are going to bring our men backwards and forwards. There is no doubt about it whatever that the Act of 19"9 interfered most materially with builders in pursuing their ordinary course of building, whereas in this case they were bound to have good customers for their land. MR. J. J. BLECK DV AND THE COST OF THE STRIKE. Mr. JOHN J. BLECKLY seconded, and said it would be in the memory of all present that his brother had been in the habit of seconding this resolution. He felt not only the lors of a life-long friend and companion, hut he missed him in connection with the work of the company, of which he had taken his half-share ; and, while he welcomed the younger men in coming to the assistance of their elders, he was afraid he could not lo tk forward to anyone to fill his brother's place so long as he (the speaker) remained in the company. He was reminded of another fact which had a melancholy signifinnce for him, and that was that he stood there as the last of those eight men c Innected with the Board who were responsible for the launching of the company in 1873. They bad all died in harness in the service of the company, except Mr. Beck, who retired some five years ago, and was still enjoying a well-earned rest. He could endorse what the Cnairman had said in regard to the report and general legislation from which the Company suffered, and would add, if allowed to intro- duce the least tinge of nolitics,that they were promised a feast of rare, refreshing fruit, but be thought if they
